The paper addresses the issue of cultural bias in text-to-image generation models, arguing that models trained on predominantly Western datasets often fail to accurately represent specific cultures, like Russian culture. The authors propose a methodology called CRAFT (Cultural Russian-Oriented Dataset Adaptation for Focused Text-to-Image Generation) for creating a culturally-aware dataset. This involves defining categories and entities within Russian culture, collecting and filtering relevant data, manually captioning images, and then using this adapted dataset to fine-tune the Kandinsky 3.1 text-to-image model. The paper reports human evaluation results that show an improvement in the model's ability to generate images that reflect Russian cultural concepts after fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting text-to-image models to better represent specific cultures is somewhat novel.  While domain adaptation and fine-tuning are well-established techniques, applying them specifically to address *cultural* understanding in visual generation is a valuable application. The concept of "cultural code" is not entirely new, but its explicit incorporation into the pipeline of text-to-image generation is a contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a relevant and potentially problematic limitation of current large-scale text-to-image models.  The lack of cultural awareness can lead to inaccurate, stereotypical, or even offensive generations.  By tackling this bias, the work contributes to making these models more inclusive and useful for a wider range of users.  The CRAFT methodology, while tailored to Russian culture, provides a blueprint for adapting models to other cultures. The increase in generation quality for Russian concepts demonstrated through human evaluation shows that the methodology has the potential to be generalized to other cultures.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of cultural bias in text-to-image generation and its potential negative consequences.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The CRAFT methodology is presented in a structured and replicable manner, with detailed steps for data collection, filtering, and captioning.
    *   **Human Evaluation:** The use of human evaluation to assess the quality of generated images adds credibility to the results.
    *   **Practical Application:**  Demonstrating the efficacy of the approach by fine-tuning Kandinsky 3.1 is a strong point.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on Russian culture and the Kandinsky 3.1 model. The generalizability of the findings needs to be explored.
    *   **Subjectivity in Data Collection and Filtering:**  Defining "Russian cultural code" and manually filtering data based on it inherently involves subjective judgments. A more rigorous framework for defining and identifying cultural relevance could strengthen the approach.
    *   **Captioning Bottleneck:** Manual captioning is time-consuming and expensive. Exploring automated captioning techniques (even with human review) or methods to leverage existing data more efficiently could be beneficial. Although the authors discuss the limitations of automatic captioning in their paper.
    *   **Evaluation Metric:** There is no automatic metric or benchmark provided to quantify the improvement. The human evaluation results are useful, however, they are limited by the amount of participants.

*   **Influence:**  The paper could influence future research by encouraging the development of more culturally sensitive AI models. It provides a practical framework for addressing cultural bias in text-to-image generation and highlights the importance of data curation and evaluation in this context.  However, the impact may be limited until the approach is validated on other languages, visual concepts, and models.

**Score:** 6.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a practical methodology for tackling cultural bias in text-to-image generation. While fine-tuning itself is not a new concept, the cultural adaptation of the dataset is. The paper demonstrates tangible improvements in generation quality for a specific culture. The weaknesses, particularly the scope limitation to Russian culture, the dependence on subjective data filtering, and manual captioning, hold back a higher score. A further limitation stems from the limited data provided for human evaluation, a bigger data pool or more rigorous evaluation metrics would add greater credibility.

However, the novelty of the application, the clear articulation of the problem, the structured methodology, and the positive human evaluation results justify a score above average. The CRAFT methodology has the potential to be generalized to other cultures and inspire further research in this direction. A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's solid contribution while acknowledging its limitations and the need for further validation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### D-CODA: Diffusion for Coordinated Dual-Arm Data Augmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04860v1)
- **Authors**: I-Chun Arthur Liu, Jason Chen, Gaurav Sukhatme, Daniel Seita
- **Abstract**: Learning bimanual manipulation is challenging due to its high dimensionality and tight coordination required between two arms. Eye-in-hand imitation learning, which uses wrist-mounted cameras, simplifies perception by focusing on task-relevant views. However, collecting diverse demonstrations remains costly, motivating the need for scalable data augmentation. While prior work has explored visual augmentation in single-arm settings, extending these approaches to bimanual manipulation requires generating viewpoint-consistent observations across both arms and producing corresponding action labels that are both valid and feasible. In this work, we propose Diffusion for COordinated Dual-arm Data Augmentation (D-CODA), a method for offline data augmentation tailored to eye-in-hand bimanual imitation learning that trains a diffusion model to synthesize novel, viewpoint-consistent wrist-camera images for both arms while simultaneously generating joint-space action labels. It employs constrained optimization to ensure that augmented states involving gripper-to-object contacts adhere to constraints suitable for bimanual coordination. We evaluate D-CODA on 5 simulated and 3 real-world tasks. Our results across 2250 simulation trials and 300 real-world trials demonstrate that it outperforms baselines and ablations, showing its potential for scalable data augmentation in eye-in-hand bimanual manipulation. Our project website is at: https://dcodaaug.github.io/D-CODA/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "D-CODA: Diffusion for Coordinated Dual-Arm Data Augmentation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces D-CODA, a novel diffusion-based data augmentation method for bimanual manipulation learning using eye-in-hand cameras. D-CODA addresses the challenges of generating viewpoint-consistent wrist-camera images and corresponding action labels for both arms. It employs a diffusion model to synthesize novel views while simultaneously generating joint-space action labels.  A key component is a constrained optimization process that ensures augmented states involving gripper-object contacts are valid for coordinated manipulation. The method is evaluated on several simulated and real-world bimanual tasks, demonstrating improved performance compared to baselines and ablations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific application of diffusion models to data augmentation in the context of *coordinated* bimanual manipulation with eye-in-hand cameras. While diffusion models for data augmentation in robotics are not entirely new (building upon works like DMD [9]), the adaptation and refinement for bimanual coordination, including the constrained optimization for action label generation in contact-rich scenarios, represents a significant advance. The segmentation of bimanual tasks into contactless and contact-rich states using a large vision model like SAM2 to inform the type of data augmentation applied adds another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial problem in robot learning: the high cost and effort associated with collecting diverse training data, especially for complex tasks like bimanual manipulation. By providing an effective offline data augmentation technique, D-CODA has the potential to significantly reduce the data requirements for training robust bimanual manipulation policies. The improved performance in both simulation and real-world experiments underscores the practical value of the approach. The focus on eye-in-hand camera setups further increases its practicality, given the benefits of task-relevant views. The empirical results, showing improved performance in a range of simulated and real-world tasks are well documented and support the paper's claims. A weakness is the reliance on a waypoint-based motion planner for demonstration generation, which may limit the complexity of the data and the tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated solution.
    *   The constrained optimization method ensures the validity of generated actions for bimanual coordination.
    *   Strong empirical results in both simulation and real-world settings.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of different components of the proposed method.
    *   The combination of a diffusion model with SAM2 for contact state detection is innovative.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method is specifically designed for eye-in-hand camera setups and may not be directly applicable to other sensor modalities or perspectives without significant modifications.
    *   The constrained optimization process might be computationally expensive, limiting its scalability to very high-dimensional action spaces or complex constraints.
    *   While the results show benefits, the degree of improvement can vary based on the task, and one of the task showed D-CODA doing worse than the baseline with the third person camera views.
    *   Demonstrations are generated using a waypoint-based motion planner, which may not capture the full complexity of real-world human demonstrations.
    *   The success is partly due to fine tuning the chunk size, and if you select the incorrect chunk size the results can degrade significantly.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research in data augmentation for bimanual robotics by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion models and constrained optimization for generating valid and consistent training data.  It also points to the importance of considering task-specific constraints when designing data augmentation techniques for coordinated manipulation. The work provides a solid foundation for future research into combining diffusion models with other approaches, such as reinforcement learning, to achieve even more robust and adaptable bimanual manipulation skills.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** D-CODA makes a solid contribution to the field of bimanual manipulation by providing a practical and effective data augmentation technique. While it builds upon existing work on diffusion models in robotics, the specific adaptations and innovations for coordinated dual-arm manipulation, including the constraint-based action generation, are novel and significant. The thorough evaluation and clear presentation of results further strengthens the paper. However, the method's reliance on eye-in-hand cameras and the potential computational cost of constrained optimization limit its broad applicability and scalability. Also, the demonstrations are generated using a waypoint-based motion planner, which may not capture the full complexity of real-world human demonstrations. A score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contribution and potential influence on the field, while acknowledging its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### From First Draft to Final Insight: A Multi-Agent Approach for Feedback Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04869v1)
- **Authors**: Jie Cao, Chloe Qianhui Zhao, Xian Chen, Shuman Wang, Christian Schunn, Kenneth R. Koedinger, Jionghao Lin
- **Abstract**: Producing large volumes of high-quality, timely feedback poses significant challenges to instructors. To address this issue, automation technologies-particularly Large Language Models (LLMs)-show great potential. However, current LLM-based research still shows room for improvement in terms of feedback quality. Our study proposed a multi-agent approach performing "generation, evaluation, and regeneration" (G-E-RG) to further enhance feedback quality. In the first-generation phase, six methods were adopted, combining three feedback theoretical frameworks and two prompt methods: zero-shot and retrieval-augmented generation with chain-of-thought (RAG_CoT). The results indicated that, compared to first-round feedback, G-E-RG significantly improved final feedback across six methods for most dimensions. Specifically:(1) Evaluation accuracy for six methods increased by 3.36% to 12.98% (p<0.001); (2) The proportion of feedback containing four effective components rose from an average of 27.72% to an average of 98.49% among six methods, sub-dimensions of providing critiques, highlighting strengths, encouraging agency, and cultivating dialogue also showed great enhancement (p<0.001); (3) There was a significant improvement in most of the feature values (p<0.001), although some sub-dimensions (e.g., strengthening the teacher-student relationship) still require further enhancement; (4) The simplicity of feedback was effectively enhanced (p<0.001) for three methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel multi-agent "Generation, Evaluation, and Regeneration" (G-E-RG) approach to improve the quality of feedback generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The process involves an initial feedback generation phase using different prompt engineering techniques and feedback theoretical frameworks, followed by an automated evaluation of the generated feedback using LLMs, and finally, a regeneration phase where the feedback is refined based on the evaluation results.  The authors compare various prompt engineering and framework combinations and demonstrate that the G-E-RG approach leads to significant improvements in feedback quality across several dimensions, including accuracy, completeness, and simplicity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The G-E-RG approach presents a relatively novel iterative methodology for improving LLM-generated feedback. While individual components like using LLMs for feedback and prompt engineering are not new, the multi-agent iterative loop combining generation, automated evaluation, and regeneration is a worthwhile contribution. The comparison of different prompt engineering strategies in conjunction with feedback frameworks in the *initial* generation phase provides interesting insights, as well.

*   **Significance:**  High-quality, timely feedback is a critical element in effective learning, yet providing it at scale is challenging. The paper directly addresses this by attempting to automate and improve the process using LLMs. The empirical results, showcasing improvements in key dimensions of feedback quality, suggest the potential for the G-E-RG method to alleviate some of the burden on instructors and enhance student learning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study employs a well-defined rubric for evaluating feedback quality, covering reliability, components, and features. The use of both human and LLM-based evaluation adds robustness.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents quantitative results demonstrating statistically significant improvements in feedback quality after applying the G-E-RG approach.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology is described in sufficient detail, allowing for potential replication.
    *   **Focus on Learner-Centered Feedback:** The framework emphasizes key pedagogical elements such as self-regulation and independent learning, which can contribute to a shift from teacher-directed to learner-focused models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Context Specificity:** The data is collected from a single graduate course, which might limit the generalizability of the findings to other domains or educational levels.
    *   **LLM Evaluation Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of LLMs in reliably assessing certain feedback features (F2-usable, F5-independence). Reliance on LLM evaluation, while practical, introduces a potential source of bias.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Human Feedback:** While the goal is to improve *automated* feedback, comparing the G-E-RG generated feedback to *human* generated feedback would provide a stronger benchmark and contextualize the gains achieved. Without this, it's hard to gauge how close the automated feedback comes to truly high-quality human feedback.
    *   **Limited Depth on Computational Cost:** Though the paper alludes to resource intensiveness in Discussion, detailed quantitative computational analysis of the multi-agent pipeline should be presented.

*   **Potential Impact:** If validated across diverse contexts, the G-E-RG approach could be a valuable tool for educators seeking to provide effective feedback at scale. It has the potential to inform the design of automated feedback systems and improve learning outcomes.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper presents a promising approach and demonstrates empirical improvements, it has limitations regarding generalizability, LLM evaluation biases, a lack of comparison against human-generated feedback, and absence of quantitative computational cost information.  These weaknesses temper the significance of the findings.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### GroverGPT-2: Simulating Grover's Algorithm via Chain-of-Thought Reasoning and Quantum-Native Tokenization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04880v1)
- **Authors**: Min Chen, Jinglei Cheng, Pingzhi Li, Haoran Wang, Tianlong Chen, Junyu Liu
- **Abstract**: Quantum computing offers theoretical advantages over classical computing for specific tasks, yet the boundary of practical quantum advantage remains an open question. To investigate this boundary, it is crucial to understand whether, and how, classical machines can learn and simulate quantum algorithms. Recent progress in large language models (LLMs) has demonstrated strong reasoning abilities, prompting exploration into their potential for this challenge. In this work, we introduce GroverGPT-2, an LLM-based method for simulating Grover's algorithm using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning and quantum-native tokenization. Building on its predecessor, GroverGPT-2 performs simulation directly from quantum circuit representations while producing logically structured and interpretable outputs. Our results show that GroverGPT-2 can learn and internalize quantum circuit logic through efficient processing of quantum-native tokens, providing direct evidence that classical models like LLMs can capture the structure of quantum algorithms. Furthermore, GroverGPT-2 outputs interleave circuit data with natural language, embedding explicit reasoning into the simulation. This dual capability positions GroverGPT-2 as a prototype for advancing machine understanding of quantum algorithms and modeling quantum circuit logic. We also identify an empirical scaling law for GroverGPT-2 with increasing qubit numbers, suggesting a path toward scalable classical simulation. These findings open new directions for exploring the limits of classical simulatability, enhancing quantum education and research, and laying groundwork for future foundation models in quantum computing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GroverGPT-2: Simulating Grover's Algorithm via Chain-of-Thought Reasoning and Quantum-Native Tokenization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GroverGPT-2, an LLM-based approach for classically simulating Grover's algorithm. GroverGPT-2 builds upon its predecessor (GroverGPT) by directly processing quantum circuit representations (QASM) and generating structured, interpretable outputs through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. The method also incorporates quantum-native tokenization to improve the LLM's understanding of quantum circuit structure. The authors demonstrate that GroverGPT-2 can learn and internalize quantum circuit logic, outputting circuit data interleaved with natural language reasoning steps. They also investigate the scaling properties of GroverGPT-2 with increasing qubit numbers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers moderate novelty. Building upon GroverGPT, it enhances the method with CoT reasoning and quantum-native tokenization. While using LLMs for quantum simulation is not entirely new, the combination of these techniques and the focus on direct QASM input (without natural language prompts) represent a solid incremental improvement. The empirical scaling law exploration also provides some new insight.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in exploring the capabilities and limitations of classical machines (LLMs) to understand and simulate quantum algorithms. GroverGPT-2 serves as a prototype for bridging the gap between quantum and classical computing domains and demonstrates the potential of LLMs to model quantum circuit logic. This can have implications for machine understanding of quantum algorithms. The observation of an empirical scaling law, while preliminary, is a potentially interesting finding that could guide further research on scalable classical quantum simulation. It also can potentially guide quantum education tool development.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Direct QASM input:** Moving away from natural language prompts and focusing on direct QASM input is a strength, as it provides a more realistic and controlled experimental setting.
    *   **Chain-of-Thought reasoning:** Incorporating CoT is beneficial, as it sheds light on how the LLM is processing and reasoning about the quantum circuit, adding interpretability to the simulation.
    *   **Quantum-native tokenization:** The tokenization scheme seems well-designed and justified, improving the LLM's efficiency in processing QASM code. The empirical evidence provided indicates the quantum-native tokenizer helps to reduce the sequence length.
    *   **Scalability Exploration:**  The experiments exploring the performance of GroverGPT-2 with varying qubit numbers and training data scaling attempts to address an important question about the method's scalability.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of ablation study to test components of the model contributes to a strong analysis of different components impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Qubit Numbers:** The experiments are constrained by relatively low qubit numbers (up to 13 with oracle input, and much lower when considering full circuit input due to context length constraints). This limits the claims that can be made about scaling to larger quantum systems.
    *   **Grover's Algorithm Specificity:** The study is limited to Grover's algorithm. While the principles may be generalizable, more evidence is needed to show that the approach can be applied to other quantum algorithms of varying complexity.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** While the empirical scaling law is interesting, a more theoretical analysis of the LLM's computational complexity in relation to the quantum circuit's complexity would be beneficial.
    *   **Reliance on Supervised Fine-Tuning:** The model relies heavily on supervised fine-tuning with high-quality CoT data. Generating this data may be challenging for more complex quantum algorithms, limiting the method's broader applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence research in the following areas:
    *   Classical simulation of quantum algorithms.
    *   Applications of LLMs in quantum computing.
    *   Machine understanding of quantum circuits.
    *   Design of quantum education tool using LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile incremental contribution to the field of quantum computing and machine learning. While not groundbreaking, it provides solid evidence of the capabilities of LLMs to simulate and "understand" quantum algorithms and identifies some promising avenues for future research. The limitations relating to qubit numbers and algorithm specificity, along with the reliance on supervised data, reduce the score. Also the lack of robust theoretical analysis of complexity limits the influence of the study.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: quant-ph
- **Score**: 6/10

### ConCISE: Confidence-guided Compression in Step-by-step Efficient Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04881v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqing Qiao, Yongheng Deng, Jiali Zeng, Dong Wang, Lai Wei, Fandong Meng, Jie Zhou, Ju Ren, Yaoxue Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) perform strongly in complex reasoning tasks via Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, but often suffer from verbose outputs caused by redundant content, increasing computational overhead, and degrading user experience. Existing compression methods either operate post-hoc pruning, risking disruption to reasoning coherence, or rely on sampling-based selection, which fails to intervene effectively during generation. In this work, we introduce a confidence-guided perspective to explain the emergence of redundant reflection in LRMs, identifying two key patterns: Confidence Deficit, where the model reconsiders correct steps due to low internal confidence, and Termination Delay, where reasoning continues even after reaching a confident answer. Based on this analysis, we propose ConCISE (Confidence-guided Compression In Step-by-step Efficient Reasoning), a framework that simplifies reasoning chains by reinforcing the model's confidence during inference, thus preventing the generation of redundant reflection steps. It integrates Confidence Injection to stabilize intermediate steps and Early Stopping to terminate reasoning when confidence is sufficient. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning LRMs on ConCISE-generated data yields significantly shorter outputs, reducing length by up to approximately 50% under SimPO, while maintaining high task accuracy. ConCISE consistently outperforms existing baselines across multiple reasoning benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ConCISE: Confidence-guided Compression in Step-by-step Efficient Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CONCISE, a novel framework for compressing reasoning chains generated by Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) while preserving task accuracy. It addresses the problem of verbose outputs in LRMs, which stem from redundant reflections due to two key patterns: *Confidence Deficit* (models reconsidering correct steps due to low confidence) and *Termination Delay* (models continuing reasoning after a confident answer is reached).  CONCISE integrates *Confidence Injection* to stabilize intermediate steps and *Early Stopping* to terminate reasoning when sufficient confidence is achieved.  The authors fine-tune LRMs using data generated by CONCISE via both SFT and SimPO, demonstrating significant reductions in output length (up to ~50% under SimPO) while maintaining or improving task accuracy across various reasoning benchmarks. The paper argues that CONCISE proactively suppresses redundant reflections, leading to more efficient and compact reasoning chains compared to existing methods that rely on post-hoc pruning or sampling-based selection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its confidence-guided perspective on reasoning chain compression. Existing methods often focus on correctness or brevity, whereas CONCISE explicitly models and addresses the *reasons* for redundancy, targeting the model's internal confidence. The identification of Confidence Deficit and Termination Delay as specific patterns contributing to verbosity is also a valuable contribution. The integration of Confidence Injection and Early Stopping, while conceptually straightforward, provides a practical and effective mechanism for proactive compression. In essence, it moves compression from a post-processing step (pruning) or data selection issue (sampling), to an inherent part of the inference process.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to make LRMs more practical for resource-constrained environments and improve user experience. Reducing the verbosity of reasoning chains directly addresses key limitations of current LRMs, such as computational overhead and latency. Demonstrating robust performance across multiple reasoning benchmarks, along with successful generalization to out-of-domain tasks, strengthens the argument that CONCISE can be broadly applicable. The ablation studies effectively highlight the contributions of each component (Confidence Injection and Early Stopping).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of verbose outputs in LRMs and identifies the underlying causes (Confidence Deficit and Termination Delay).
    *   **Principled Approach:**  CONCISE provides a principled way to mitigate redundancy by actively shaping the model's reasoning process based on confidence, rather than relying solely on post-hoc methods or data selection.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on multiple benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of CONCISE in terms of both compression and accuracy.
    *   **Strong Ablation Study:** The Ablation study successfully shows that confidence injection and early stopping are each necessary for the best performance, highlighting that they each target different types of redundancy.
    *   **Effective Generalization:** The generalization to out-of-domain datasets, particularly GPQA\_diamond, showcases the robustness and adaptability of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Hand-Crafted Confidence Phrases:** The Confidence Injection component relies on a manually curated pool of confidence phrases. While the authors conduct experiments to refine this pool, it introduces a potential source of bias and limits the framework's adaptability to different models or tasks. Developing a more automated way to generate or select confidence phrases could improve the framework.
    *   **Model Dependency:** The effectiveness of the Early Stopping mechanism and the optimal threshold (te) may be sensitive to the specific LRM architecture and scale.  The authors acknowledge this limitation, but further investigation into the framework's behavior across diverse LRMs would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Intra-Step Compression:** The paper focuses primarily on reducing the number of steps in the reasoning chain but doesn't address the potential for compressing individual steps (intra-step compression). As the authors note, exploring techniques like TokenSkip alongside CONCISE could further improve compression ratios. While acknowledged in the limitations, this could significantly increase compression performance.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    *   It encourages a shift in focus from post-hoc compression to proactive methods that shape the reasoning process itself.
    *   It highlights the importance of modeling and addressing the underlying causes of redundancy in LRMs.
    *   It provides a practical framework that can be used to improve the efficiency and usability of LRMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite some limitations, CONCISE presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LRM compression. The confidence-guided perspective offers valuable insights into the origins of redundancy, and the proposed framework demonstrates impressive results across multiple benchmarks and settings. While the reliance on hand-crafted confidence phrases and potential model dependency are weaknesses, the paper's strengths outweigh these limitations. CONCISE addresses a critical problem in LRM research and provides a promising direction for future work.  Therefore, a score of 8 is justified because the paper presents significant novelty, strong empirical validation, and the potential for considerable impact on the field, while also acknowledging and discussing potential limitations.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SpatialPrompting: Keyframe-driven Zero-Shot Spatial Reasoning with Off-the-Shelf Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04911v1)
- **Authors**: Shun Taguchi, Hideki Deguchi, Takumi Hamazaki, Hiroyuki Sakai
- **Abstract**: This study introduces SpatialPrompting, a novel framework that harnesses the emergent reasoning capabilities of off-the-shelf multimodal large language models to achieve zero-shot spatial reasoning in three-dimensional (3D) environments. Unlike existing methods that rely on expensive 3D-specific fine-tuning with specialized 3D inputs such as point clouds or voxel-based features, SpatialPrompting employs a keyframe-driven prompt generation strategy. This framework uses metrics such as vision-language similarity, Mahalanobis distance, field of view, and image sharpness to select a diverse and informative set of keyframes from image sequences and then integrates them with corresponding camera pose data to effectively abstract spatial relationships and infer complex 3D structures. The proposed framework not only establishes a new paradigm for flexible spatial reasoning that utilizes intuitive visual and positional cues but also achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on benchmark datasets, such as ScanQA and SQA3D, across several metrics. The proposed method effectively eliminates the need for specialized 3D inputs and fine-tuning, offering a simpler and more scalable alternative to conventional approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SpatialPrompting, a novel framework for zero-shot spatial reasoning using multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  Instead of relying on computationally expensive 3D-specific fine-tuning with specialized inputs like point clouds or voxels, SpatialPrompting uses a keyframe-driven approach. It selects representative and informative keyframes from image sequences based on metrics like vision-language similarity, Mahalanobis distance, field of view (FOV), and image sharpness.  These keyframes, along with their corresponding camera pose data, are then used as prompts for off-the-shelf MLLMs like GPT-4o. The framework is shown to achieve state-of-the-art zero-shot performance on ScanQA and SQA3D benchmark datasets without requiring specialized 3D inputs or fine-tuning. The paper also performs ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of key components, including keyframe extraction, camera pose, and few-shot annotation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the *training-free* aspect of spatial reasoning. Prior work heavily relies on fine-tuning LLMs with 3D-specific features, which is computationally expensive and requires significant labeled data.  SpatialPrompting's clever use of keyframe selection and camera pose as prompts for *existing* MLLMs is a significant departure from this paradigm. It demonstrates that the spatial reasoning capabilities are, to some extent, already latent within pre-trained MLLMs and can be unlocked with appropriate prompting. While keyframe selection in video understanding isn't entirely new, its specific application in 3D spatial reasoning with pose information and zero-shot transfer is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The significance comes from several factors:

    *   **Scalability and Cost-Effectiveness:** By eliminating 3D-specific fine-tuning, the framework offers a simpler, more scalable, and cost-effective alternative to traditional methods. This makes spatial reasoning more accessible and practical for real-world applications.
    *   **Relevance to Robotics and Embodied AI:** Spatial reasoning is crucial for robotics and embodied AI. This paper's approach makes it easier to integrate powerful reasoning capabilities into these systems without needing to train custom models for every environment.
    *   **New Research Direction:** The paper challenges the conventional wisdom that high-dimensional, specialized inputs are necessary for spatial understanding. This could inspire new research into how MLLMs can be effectively leveraged for other perception tasks using clever prompting strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong experimental results, achieving state-of-the-art zero-shot performance.
    *   Well-designed ablation studies that clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component.
    *   Good qualitative results that showcase the framework's ability to handle diverse spatial reasoning tasks.
    *   Clear and well-written paper with a strong introduction, motivation, and discussion.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on the quality of the underlying MLLM.  The performance of SpatialPrompting is inherently limited by the capabilities of the chosen MLLM (GPT-4o in this case). This may not scale well as tasks demand more nuanced or complex reasoning.
    *   The method involves hyperparameter tuning (the alpha and beta parameters in keyframe selection and sensitivity to number of keyframes as shown in the supplementary materials). In order to adapt this framework, a user may need to be willing to tune for their specific application.
    *   Failure Analysis reveals limitations such as miscounting objects. While this is acknowledged in the paper, a more robust approach to object counting could further improve performance. The directional errors related to the user context also suggest a dependency on high quality pose estimation relative to the user.
    *   In some instances, results indicated the baseline version of GPT-40 showed the ability to solve some spatial inference problems. This could indicate that results are more dependent on base models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in the following areas:

    *   Prompt engineering for spatial reasoning.
    *   Zero-shot learning for robotics and embodied AI.
    *   Leveraging MLLMs for perception tasks.
    *   Developing more efficient and scalable spatial reasoning systems.
    *   Hybrid approaches combining keyframe-based prompting with specialized modules for object counting and user context understanding.

**Score Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of spatial reasoning. Its training-free approach, strong experimental results, and clear demonstration of the contribution of different components make it a valuable addition to the literature. While there are some limitations, the paper challenges the conventional wisdom and opens up new research directions. The paper is well-written and easy to understand. Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and the potential influence of the paper, I assign it a score of:

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GlyphMastero: A Glyph Encoder for High-Fidelity Scene Text Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04915v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Wang, Ting Liu, Xiaochao Qu, Chengjing Wu, Luoqi Liu, Xiaolin Hu
- **Abstract**: Scene text editing, a subfield of image editing, requires modifying texts in images while preserving style consistency and visual coherence with the surrounding environment. While diffusion-based methods have shown promise in text generation, they still struggle to produce high-quality results. These methods often generate distorted or unrecognizable characters, particularly when dealing with complex characters like Chinese. In such systems, characters are composed of intricate stroke patterns and spatial relationships that must be precisely maintained. We present GlyphMastero, a specialized glyph encoder designed to guide the latent diffusion model for generating texts with stroke-level precision. Our key insight is that existing methods, despite using pretrained OCR models for feature extraction, fail to capture the hierarchical nature of text structures - from individual strokes to stroke-level interactions to overall character-level structure. To address this, our glyph encoder explicitly models and captures the cross-level interactions between local-level individual characters and global-level text lines through our novel glyph attention module. Meanwhile, our model implements a feature pyramid network to fuse the multi-scale OCR backbone features at the global-level. Through these cross-level and multi-scale fusions, we obtain more detailed glyph-aware guidance, enabling precise control over the scene text generation process. Our method achieves an 18.02\% improvement in sentence accuracy over the state-of-the-art multi-lingual scene text editing baseline, while simultaneously reducing the text-region Fr\'echet inception distance by 53.28\%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GlyphMastero, a novel glyph encoder designed to improve the fidelity of scene text editing.  The core idea is that current diffusion-based text editing methods, particularly when dealing with complex characters (like Chinese), struggle to maintain stroke-level precision and style consistency. GlyphMastero addresses this by explicitly modeling the hierarchical structure of text, from individual strokes to character-level structure, and finally to the whole text line. It utilizes a glyph attention module to capture cross-level interactions and a feature pyramid network (FPN) to fuse multi-scale OCR features.  The paper demonstrates that GlyphMastero, when used with an inpainting-based diffusion framework, achieves substantial improvements in sentence accuracy and reduces Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) compared to state-of-the-art methods, particularly in multi-lingual settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core contribution lies in the *explicit hierarchical modeling of glyph structure*. While prior work utilizes OCR features as guidance, GlyphMastero's trainable glyph encoder, coupled with the glyph attention module and FPN, represents a step towards a more structured and nuanced understanding of textual elements.  The novelty isn't groundbreaking, as components like attention and FPN are well-established. However, the *specific combination and application within scene text editing, particularly with a focus on stroke-level precision,* is relatively novel.
* **Significance:** The results demonstrate tangible improvements in both text accuracy and style preservation, *especially for complex scripts* such as Chinese. This has practical implications for applications requiring high-fidelity text manipulation. Reducing distortions and improving readability of complex characters makes a valuable contribution. The large improvement in the FID score, indicating better style consistency, is also significant.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper provides a clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    * The GlyphMastero architecture is well-explained and justified.
    * The experimental results are thorough, including quantitative comparisons with strong baselines and qualitative examples showing the advantages of the method.
    * The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component of the proposed architecture.
    * The paper addresses a real-world challenge with practical implications.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the improvement in handling complex characters is a highlight, the methodology relies on a pre-trained OCR model. This introduces a dependence on the OCR model's performance and bias.  The paper would have benefited from a discussion regarding the robustness of GlyphMastero to variations in OCR accuracy.
    * The results show the method still struggles with long text sequences. This limitation reduces the applicability in scenarios where substantial text replacement is needed.
    * The reliance on an inpainting-based diffusion model as a backbone is not novel and inherits the limitations of that approach (e.g., computational cost).
    * The improvements in style similarity are smaller than those in content accuracy, suggesting that there might be room for further improvement in that area.
    * The method primarily tackles structural accuracy, and better visual coherence with the surrounding environment remains an area for improvement.

**Justification for Score:**

GlyphMastero presents a worthwhile, but not revolutionary, advancement in scene text editing. The explicit hierarchical modeling of glyphs is a novel and effective approach that yields improved results.  However, the reliance on a pre-trained OCR model and the limitations with long text sequences constrain its immediate impact. The novelty is incremental rather than disruptive, and the core building blocks (attention, FPN) are borrowed from other areas. Still, the improvement in handling complex characters, a common bottleneck in text editing tasks, is compelling. Overall, the impact is solid, but not paradigm-shifting.

Score: 7.0

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Perception, Reason, Think, and Plan: A Survey on Large Multimodal Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04921v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxin Li, Zhenyu Liu, Zitao Li, Xuanyu Zhang, Zhenran Xu, Xinyu Chen, Haoyuan Shi, Shenyuan Jiang, Xintong Wang, Jifang Wang, Shouzheng Huang, Xinping Zhao, Borui Jiang, Lanqing Hong, Longyue Wang, Zhuotao Tian, Baoxing Huai, Wenhan Luo, Weihua Luo, Zheng Zhang, Baotian Hu, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: Reasoning lies at the heart of intelligence, shaping the ability to make decisions, draw conclusions, and generalize across domains. In artificial intelligence, as systems increasingly operate in open, uncertain, and multimodal environments, reasoning becomes essential for enabling robust and adaptive behavior. Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm, integrating modalities such as text, images, audio, and video to support complex reasoning capabilities and aiming to achieve comprehensive perception, precise understanding, and deep reasoning. As research advances, multimodal reasoning has rapidly evolved from modular, perception-driven pipelines to unified, language-centric frameworks that offer more coherent cross-modal understanding. While instruction tuning and reinforcement learning have improved model reasoning, significant challenges remain in omni-modal generalization, reasoning depth, and agentic behavior. To address these issues, we present a comprehensive and structured survey of multimodal reasoning research, organized around a four-stage developmental roadmap that reflects the field's shifting design philosophies and emerging capabilities. First, we review early efforts based on task-specific modules, where reasoning was implicitly embedded across stages of representation, alignment, and fusion. Next, we examine recent approaches that unify reasoning into multimodal LLMs, with advances such as Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) and multimodal reinforcement learning enabling richer and more structured reasoning chains. Finally, drawing on empirical insights from challenging benchmarks and experimental cases of OpenAI O3 and O4-mini, we discuss the conceptual direction of native large multimodal reasoning models (N-LMRMs), which aim to support scalable, agentic, and adaptive reasoning and planning in complex, real-world environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a survey on Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs). It organizes existing research into a four-stage roadmap:
1.  Perception-Driven Modular Reasoning (task-specific modules).
2.  Language-Centric Short Reasoning (System-1, prompt-based).
3.  Language-Centric Long Reasoning (System-2, extended chains).
4.  Native Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (N-LMRMs).

The survey reviews models, architectures, learning methods, datasets, and benchmarks associated with each stage. It discusses challenges like omnimodal generalization, reasoning depth, and agentic behavior. The paper introduces the concept of N-LMRMs, focusing on native omnimodal perception, goal-driven cognition, unified representations, data synthesis, and learning from world experience. Finally, it identifies potential directions for advancing multimodal intelligence beyond current architectural constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in providing a comprehensive roadmap of the evolution of multimodal reasoning, connecting early modular designs with current LLM-based models and future N-LMRMs.  The four-stage organization is helpful in understanding the development path and the shift in design philosophies. While it builds upon existing research, the synthesis, structured organization, and the specific emphasis on reinforcement learning-enhanced reasoning in multimodal settings are valuable. The notion of "Native Large Multimodal Reasoning Models" is forward-looking, highlighting a potential paradigm shift rather than simply extrapolating from existing architectures.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical need for a coherent framework in the rapidly evolving field of multimodal reasoning. By categorizing and analyzing various approaches, it helps researchers understand the current landscape and identify gaps. The discussion of limitations (generalization, depth, agentic behavior) and the projection towards N-LMRMs provide a valuable direction for future research. The updated dataset and benchmark compilation is a helpful resource for the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive scope: Covers a wide range of models and techniques.
    *   Structured Organization: The four-stage roadmap is clear and insightful.
    *   Future-oriented:  Proposes the N-LMRM concept, anticipating trends.
    *   Up-to-date: includes recent advancements in the field.
    *   Helpful resources: Dataset and benchmark summary table.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Somewhat descriptive:  While comprehensive, the survey is primarily descriptive. A more in-depth critical comparison of specific models within each stage (beyond a high-level categorization) could strengthen the analysis.
    *   N-LMRM concept is high-level: The N-LMRM section outlines general principles. More concrete architectural or training proposals would enhance its immediate impact.
    *   Limited quantitative analysis: More quantitative analysis of benchmark performance (beyond general mentions of "improvements") could further solidify the arguments.

*   **Potential Influence:** This survey has the potential to guide future research directions by clearly articulating the limitations of current LMRMs and providing a framework for developing more capable and adaptable reasoning systems. The emphasis on learning from world experience and creating more agentic models could be particularly influential.

**Justification for the score:**

I'm assigning a score of **8**.

*   **Rationale:** The paper offers a significant contribution by synthesizing a large body of work and providing a structured roadmap for understanding multimodal reasoning models. The forward-looking perspective on N-LMRMs is valuable. The limitations outlined above prevent it from achieving a higher score, particularly the high-level nature of N-LMRMs without a specific implementation/architecture proposal. However, it presents a strong foundation for understanding the direction of research, and a detailed analysis is carried out across these multiple reasoning models. Overall, the paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of multimodal reasoning.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Accurate and Fast Channel Estimation for Fluid Antenna Systems with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04930v1)
- **Authors**: Erqiang Tang, Wei Guo, Hengtao He, Shenghui Song, Jun Zhang, Khaled B. Letaief
- **Abstract**: Fluid antenna systems (FAS) offer enhanced spatial diversity for next-generation wireless systems. However, acquiring accurate channel state information (CSI) remains challenging due to the large number of reconfigurable ports and the limited availability of radio-frequency (RF) chains -- particularly in high-dimensional FAS scenarios. To address this challenge, we propose an efficient posterior sampling-based channel estimator that leverages a diffusion model (DM) with a simplified U-Net architecture to capture the spatial correlation structure of two-dimensional FAS channels. The DM is initially trained offline in an unsupervised way and then applied online as a learned implicit prior to reconstruct CSI from partial observations via posterior sampling through a denoising diffusion restoration model (DDRM). To accelerate the online inference, we introduce a skipped sampling strategy that updates only a subset of latent variables during the sampling process, thereby reducing the computational cost with minimal accuracy degradation. Simulation results demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves significantly higher estimation accuracy and over 20x speedup compared to state-of-the-art compressed sensing-based methods, highlighting its potential for practical deployment in high-dimensional FAS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel channel estimation technique for Fluid Antenna Systems (FAS) using diffusion models (DMs). Recognizing the challenges of high dimensionality and limited RF chains in FAS, the authors leverage DMs to capture the spatial correlation of FAS channels.  A simplified U-Net architecture is used to train the DM offline in an unsupervised manner.  Online, the trained DM serves as a learned prior for reconstructing CSI from partial observations via a denoising diffusion restoration model (DDRM). To accelerate inference, a skipped sampling strategy is introduced, updating only a subset of latent variables during the sampling process.  Simulation results demonstrate significant improvements in estimation accuracy and speed compared to compressed sensing-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of diffusion models to channel estimation in FAS is a relatively novel approach. While DMs have been explored in other wireless communication contexts, their use to exploit spatial correlations in FAS channels, coupled with a specific U-Net architecture tailored for this application, presents a unique contribution. The skipped sampling technique for accelerating the inference process further enhances the practical value of the approach. However, DMs are becoming increasingly popular, so the general concept of applying DMs to wireless problems is less novel than it might have been a year or two ago. The specific U-Net architecture and its adaptation, along with the skipped sampling, are where the incremental novelty lies.

*   **Significance:** Accurate channel estimation is crucial for the performance of FAS. The proposed method addresses a key challenge in realizing the potential of FAS, particularly in high-dimensional scenarios. The reported performance gains, both in terms of accuracy and speed, are significant and demonstrate the practical applicability of the approach. If validated in real-world experiments, the method could contribute to more efficient and robust wireless communication systems using FAS. However, the method requires offline training, which increases complexity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Performance:**  The paper presents convincing simulation results showing superior performance compared to benchmark algorithms. The gains are substantial.
    *   **Efficiency:** The skipped sampling method effectively reduces computational complexity without sacrificing accuracy.
    *   **Well-explained Methodology:** The approach is clearly described, including the DM architecture, training process, and posterior sampling strategy.
    *   **Problem Importance:** Addresses a key challenge hindering the adoption of FAS technology.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simulation-based Evaluation:** The evaluation is based solely on simulations. Real-world channel characteristics are often more complex and dynamic than those captured in simulations. Field experiments would be necessary to fully validate the robustness and practicality of the approach.
    *   **Computational Cost of Offline Training:** While the online inference is accelerated, the offline training of the DM can be computationally expensive, although this can be seen as an upfront cost. The paper doesn't extensively discuss the resource requirements for training, especially as the size of the FAS grows.
    *   **Limited Comparison Scope:** While comparing to CS-based methods is reasonable, comparisons to other modern channel estimation techniques might be beneficial.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** DMs often have several hyperparameters that can significantly impact performance. The paper could benefit from a discussion of the sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and implementation of FAS in future wireless communication systems. If the method proves robust in real-world environments, it could enable wider adoption of FAS technology. The combination of a learned prior (DM) with posterior sampling is a powerful approach that could be applied to other channel estimation problems as well.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to channel estimation in FAS, backed by strong simulation results. The skipped sampling optimization is also a good contribution. While the dependence on simulation-based validation and the absence of detailed training resource analysis are limitations, the significance of the performance gains and the potential impact of the method justify a score of 7. The method is relevant, and the numerical results are compelling. The authors are well-known in the area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Based LLMs for Position Bias-Aware Reranking in Personalized Recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04948v1)
- **Authors**: Md Aminul Islam, Ahmed Sayeed Faruk
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems are essential for delivering personalized content across digital platforms by modeling user preferences and behaviors. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been adopted for prompt-based recommendation due to their ability to generate personalized outputs without task-specific training. However, LLM-based methods face limitations such as limited context window size, inefficient pointwise and pairwise prompting, and difficulty handling listwise ranking due to token constraints. LLMs can also be sensitive to position bias, as they may overemphasize earlier items in the prompt regardless of their true relevance. To address and investigate these issues, we propose a hybrid framework that combines a traditional recommendation model with an LLM for reranking top-k items using structured prompts. We evaluate the effects of user history reordering and instructional prompts for mitigating position bias. Experiments on MovieLens-100K show that randomizing user history improves ranking quality, but LLM-based reranking does not outperform the base model. Explicit instructions to reduce position bias are also ineffective. Our evaluations reveal limitations in LLMs' ability to model ranking context and mitigate bias. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/aminul7506/LLMForReRanking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) for personalized recommendations through a hybrid framework. The framework combines a traditional recommender system (LightGCN) to generate candidate items with an LLM (GPT-4o) for reranking. The authors investigate the impact of user history ordering and instructional prompts on mitigating position bias, a common issue in recommender systems. Experiments on the MovieLens-100K dataset reveal that randomizing user history improves ranking quality, but LLM-based reranking does not outperform the base model. Explicit instructions to reduce position bias are also found to be ineffective, suggesting limitations in LLMs' ability to model ranking context and address bias effectively in this setting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs for reranking in a hybrid recommendation system is not entirely new, but the paper offers a focused investigation into the specific problem of position bias within this framework. The evaluation of different prompting strategies, especially regarding history ordering and explicit bias-reduction instructions, adds a layer of novelty to the work. The investigation of limitations, as well as opportunities, is valuable for the Recommender Systems community.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant challenge in LLM-based recommendation systems: how to mitigate biases that can arise from the prompt construction and model behavior. Position bias is a well-studied problem in traditional recommender systems, and its potential amplification in LLM-based approaches warrants investigation. The negative results, while perhaps disappointing, are informative for the community, highlighting areas where LLMs struggle and indicating directions for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and proposes a well-structured hybrid framework.
    *   The experimental setup is sound, using a benchmark dataset and standard evaluation metrics.
    *   The investigation into different prompting strategies is insightful, revealing the limitations of naive approaches for bias mitigation.
    *   The discussion of the results is thorough and provides valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities of using LLMs for recommendation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are limited to a single dataset (MovieLens-100K) and a specific LLM (GPT-4o).  The generalizability of the findings may be limited. It would be beneficial to see this approach tested on more datasets, possibly including richer contextual data, and with other LLMs.
    *   The prompts used are relatively simple. More sophisticated prompt engineering techniques, such as chain-of-thought or incorporating user and item embeddings, could potentially improve the results.
    *   The paper doesn't explore fine-tuning the LLM for the specific ranking task or for position bias mitigation, which could have been a more promising avenue for improvement.
    *   The lack of improvement compared to the baseline is concerning and suggests that the current method of integration may not be optimal.

*   **Impact:** While the results are negative, the paper's identification of limitations and exploration of prompt strategies can guide future research in this area. The emphasis on position bias and its interaction with LLMs is timely and relevant. It provides a starting point for developing more effective and robust LLM-based recommendation systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a useful contribution by highlighting the challenges of using LLMs to improve recommendation systems, particularly when dealing with position bias. The negative results are valuable because they identify the limitations of naive prompting approaches and show where more research is needed. However, the limited scope of the experiments and the lack of significant improvements compared to the baseline limit the overall impact and novelty. The research opens the field towards the limitations and areas for improvement, rather than presenting a groundbreaking solution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Position: Epistemic Artificial Intelligence is Essential for Machine Learning Models to Know When They Do Not Know
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04950v1)
- **Authors**: Shireen Kudukkil Manchingal, Fabio Cuzzolin
- **Abstract**: Despite the impressive achievements of AI, including advancements in generative models and large language models, there remains a significant gap in the ability of AI to handle uncertainty and generalize beyond the training data. We argue that AI models, especially in autonomous systems, fail to make robust predictions when faced with unfamiliar or adversarial data, as evidenced by incidents with autonomous vehicles. Traditional machine learning approaches struggle to address these issues due to an overemphasis on data fitting and domain adaptation. This position paper posits a paradigm shift towards epistemic artificial intelligence, emphasizing the need for models to learn not only from what they know but also from their ignorance. This approach, which focuses on recognizing and managing uncertainty, offers a potential solution to improve the resilience and robustness of AI systems, ensuring that they can better handle unpredictable real-world environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This position paper argues for a paradigm shift in machine learning towards what the authors term "Epistemic Artificial Intelligence" (EAI). They contend that current AI models, especially those used in autonomous systems and large language models (LLMs), are limited by their inability to handle uncertainty, leading to failures in unfamiliar or adversarial environments.  The core concept of EAI is to design models that explicitly learn from their ignorance, adhering to the Socratic principle of "knowing that you do not know." This involves representing and managing uncertainty, particularly epistemic uncertainty (reducible with more information), through methods like credal sets and random sets. The authors believe this approach will improve the robustness, resilience, and reliability of AI systems, especially in safety-critical applications like autonomous driving, climate change prediction, and pandemic response. The paper contrasts EAI with traditional machine learning methods, Bayesian approaches, ensemble methods, and evidential methods, highlighting the limitations of each in capturing epistemic uncertainty. It also discusses potential applications of EAI in supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning scenarios, as well as in generative AI and LLMs, and touches upon the challenges and opportunities associated with this paradigm shift.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in advocating for a particular framing of the uncertainty problem in AI, specifically emphasizing "learning from ignorance" and positioning EAI as a distinct paradigm. While the individual components (e.g., credal sets, random sets) are not new, the explicit focus on epistemic uncertainty as the *driving force* behind model design and the synthesis of these components into a coherent vision of EAI is what offers a degree of novelty. The framing of the "know that you do not know" aspect as a core principle is also a valuable perspective.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is considerable. The lack of robustness and generalization in AI systems is a major obstacle to their widespread and safe deployment, especially in critical applications. The paper correctly identifies the limitations of existing approaches in addressing this issue, particularly the tendency to "average out" or misrepresent epistemic uncertainty. If the EAI paradigm can lead to the development of more reliable and trustworthy AI systems, it could have a significant impact on various fields.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current AI systems in handling uncertainty and the consequences of these limitations.
    *   **Strong Motivation:** The authors convincingly argue the need for a paradigm shift towards explicit uncertainty representation and management.
    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** The paper provides a broad overview of different uncertainty quantification methods and their limitations.
    *   **Well-Structured Argument:** The paper's arguments are logically presented, and the key concepts are well-defined.
    *   The inclusion of comparison in terms of time complexity is beneficial to the reader.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Concrete Examples:** While the paper proposes the EAI paradigm, it lacks detailed examples of how this paradigm can be translated into specific model architectures and training algorithms for a wide range of application. The results are still limited.
    *   **Overclaims/Oversimplifications:** The paper sometimes oversimplifies the limitations of existing methods. For example, while Bayesian methods do have limitations in capturing epistemic uncertainty, hierarchical Bayesian models can, to some extent, address this issue. The paper states it, but later tends to undermine it.
    *   **Lack of Rigorous Justification for the Centrality of 'Learning from Ignorance':** While the "learning from ignorance" framework offers a novel lens to examine EAI, the paper does not provide a rigorous mathematical or theoretical justification for the *centrality* of this principle. Does this imply ignoring the data we have? How does one balance learning from knowledge versus ignorance? These questions require further examination.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** The paper acknowledges scalability challenges of some uncertainty methods like those relying on IP methods (i.e. IP's computational complexity in high dimensions). Without demonstrations of how to overcome these, the practical impact of EAI is uncertain.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the EAI paradigm gains traction, it could influence the design of future AI systems, leading to more robust and trustworthy models. It could also spur further research into uncertainty quantification methods, particularly those that are scalable and effective in capturing epistemic uncertainty. It provides an exciting venue for future research.

*   **Justification:** While the individual techniques are not fundamentally new, the paper’s synthesis and emphasis on the "learning from ignorance" framework present a valuable perspective on addressing a critical problem in AI. Its limitations—lack of concrete implementation details and potential oversimplifications—temper its impact. However, it is worth noting that they were addressed by the community later, notably by the same authors (e.g. Random Set Deep Learning), in recent work.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a worthwhile perspective on the AI safety and robustness issues by framing the problem as a need to explicitly "learn from ignorance," thus prompting a call to action for the development of robust models. While the concept of addressing uncertainty is not new, the synthesis of existing techniques like IP methods to meet this demand offers an interesting starting point for future research. Its impact is limited by a lack of implementation details or theoretical support, and is more of a position paper than one with empirical results to back up its claims, and it may oversimplify the limitations of prior methods. However, the call to action is relevant, so its potential influence warrants a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chain-of-Thought Tokens are Computer Program Variables
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04955v1)
- **Authors**: Fangwei Zhu, Peiyi Wang, Zhifang Sui
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thoughts (CoT) requires large language models (LLMs) to generate intermediate steps before reaching the final answer, and has been proven effective to help LLMs solve complex reasoning tasks. However, the inner mechanism of CoT still remains largely unclear. In this paper, we empirically study the role of CoT tokens in LLMs on two compositional tasks: multi-digit multiplication and dynamic programming. While CoT is essential for solving these problems, we find that preserving only tokens that store intermediate results would achieve comparable performance. Furthermore, we observe that storing intermediate results in an alternative latent form will not affect model performance. We also randomly intervene some values in CoT, and notice that subsequent CoT tokens and the final answer would change correspondingly. These findings suggest that CoT tokens may function like variables in computer programs but with potential drawbacks like unintended shortcuts and computational complexity limits between tokens. The code and data are available at https://github.com/solitaryzero/CoTs_are_Variables.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the inner workings of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). The core hypothesis is that CoT tokens function similarly to variables in computer programs, storing intermediate values that are used in subsequent computations. The authors conduct experiments on multi-digit multiplication and dynamic programming tasks to empirically validate this hypothesis. They find that:

1.  Preserving only the CoT tokens storing intermediate results achieves comparable performance to full CoT.
2.  Intermediate values can be represented in different forms (latent embeddings) without significantly impacting performance.
3.  Intervening (modifying) the values in CoT tokens causally affects subsequent computations and the final answer, confirming their variable-like role.

The paper also observes a computational complexity limit: aggressively compressing intermediate results into latent tokens degrades performance beyond a certain point. They further explored how certain common failures, like ‘shortcut’ responses (where a model gives the answer without proper calculation) could lead to poor results if intervention occurred.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central analogy—equating CoT tokens to variables in a program—is interesting and provides a fresh perspective on CoT's mechanism.  Prior work has demonstrated CoT's effectiveness and explored latent representations, but this paper directly investigates the causal role and mutability of specific CoT tokens.  The experiments involving intervention are particularly valuable in demonstrating the causal relationship.

*   **Significance:**  If CoT tokens truly function as variables, this has important implications. It suggests that LLMs are performing some form of computation beyond mere pattern matching. This understanding could lead to more efficient CoT implementations, better control over the reasoning process, and more robust models less prone to shortcuts.  The identification of computational complexity limits and shortcut learning is also significant for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear Hypothesis:  The paper starts with a clear and testable hypothesis.
    *   Empirical Validation:  The experimental methodology is sound, using controlled tasks and carefully designed interventions. The results generally support the authors' claims.
    *   Well-Explained Limitations:  The authors acknowledge the limitations of using synthetic tasks and the need for theoretical grounding.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Synthetic Tasks:  The reliance on multi-digit arithmetic and dynamic programming, while providing excellent control, may limit the generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world reasoning scenarios. It's not clear how well this translates to tasks where the intermediate results aren't numbers.
    *   Model Size: Using a smaller model (Qwen-2.5-1.5B) can affect the generality of the insights. Larger models may exhibit different behaviors.
    *   Lack of Theoretical Justification: While empirically sound, the paper lacks strong theoretical justification for the variable analogy. The authors do mention related theoretical work on transformers as Turing complete, but a more in-depth connection to existing program execution models within neural networks would strengthen the argument.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could stimulate further research in several directions:
    *   Developing methods for identifying and manipulating "variable" tokens in more complex tasks.
    *   Investigating the neural mechanisms underlying the storage and manipulation of intermediate values in LLMs.
    *   Designing more efficient CoT strategies based on the variable analogy.
    *   Exploring the connections between CoT and traditional program execution.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and interesting analogy, and the empirical evidence supports the hypothesis. However, the limitations (synthetic tasks, model size, lack of theoretical justification) prevent a higher score. The potential impact is significant, as it could lead to a deeper understanding of LLMs and the development of more efficient and controllable reasoning methods. The paper provides valuable insights and directions for future research, making it a solid contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graffe: Graph Representation Learning via Diffusion Probabilistic Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04956v1)
- **Authors**: Dingshuo Chen, Shuchen Xue, Liuji Chen, Yingheng Wang, Qiang Liu, Shu Wu, Zhi-Ming Ma, Liang Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion probabilistic models (DPMs), widely recognized for their potential to generate high-quality samples, tend to go unnoticed in representation learning. While recent progress has highlighted their potential for capturing visual semantics, adapting DPMs to graph representation learning remains in its infancy. In this paper, we introduce Graffe, a self-supervised diffusion model proposed for graph representation learning. It features a graph encoder that distills a source graph into a compact representation, which, in turn, serves as the condition to guide the denoising process of the diffusion decoder. To evaluate the effectiveness of our model, we first explore the theoretical foundations of applying diffusion models to representation learning, proving that the denoising objective implicitly maximizes the conditional mutual information between data and its representation. Specifically, we prove that the negative logarithm of the denoising score matching loss is a tractable lower bound for the conditional mutual information.
