Empirically, we conduct a series of case studies to validate our theoretical insights. In addition, Graffe delivers competitive results under the linear probing setting on node and graph classification tasks, achieving state-of-the-art performance on 9 of the 11 real-world datasets. These findings indicate that powerful generative models, especially diffusion models, serve as an effective tool for graph representation learning.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Graffe: Graph Representation Learning via Diffusion Probabilistic Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Graffe, a novel self-supervised graph representation learning framework based on diffusion probabilistic models (DPMs). Graffe addresses the challenge of adapting DPMs, primarily known for generative tasks, to the discriminative task of graph representation learning. The framework features a graph encoder that distills a source graph into a compact representation, which then conditions a diffusion decoder's denoising process. The authors provide theoretical justification by proving that the denoising objective implicitly maximizes conditional mutual information between the data and its representation, effectively following a "Diff-InfoMax" principle. Empirically, Graffe achieves competitive or state-of-the-art performance on node and graph classification tasks across various datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its principled integration of diffusion models into graph representation learning. While DPMs have shown promise in other domains (vision), their application to graphs is less explored. Graffe offers a dedicated framework addressing the specific challenges of graph data, such as its non-Euclidean nature and the need to extract meaningful representations. The theoretical analysis connecting the denoising objective to conditional mutual information and the Diff-InfoMax principle is also a significant contribution. The specific architecture Graffe uses, namely the GNN encoder with a tailored diffusion decoder for graphs, is also a novel design. This approach significantly moves past existing methods for graph representation learning.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from several factors:
    *   **Performance:** Graffe's strong empirical results on various benchmark datasets, including achieving state-of-the-art performance on many datasets, demonstrate its practical utility. It advances the state-of-the-art in graph representation learning.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for understanding why DPMs can be effective for representation learning. This is crucial for guiding future research and development in this area. It offers insight into designing better graph representation learning models using principles from information theory.
    *   **Framework:** Graffe provides a flexible and adaptable framework that can be extended and modified for various graph-related tasks. The modular design makes it easier to experiment with different encoder architectures, diffusion decoders, and loss functions.
    *   **Bridging Generative and Discriminative Learning:** The work bridges the gap between generative and discriminative learning paradigms in the context of graph representation, leveraging the strengths of generative models for representation learning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical grounding and rigorous mathematical derivations.
    *   Comprehensive empirical evaluation on a diverse set of benchmark datasets.
    *   Clear and well-organized presentation.
    *   The Diff-InfoMax principle provides a novel perspective on the relationship between denoising objectives and mutual information maximization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experimental results are compelling, more detailed analysis into why Graffe performs particularly well on some datasets but not others would be useful. This requires analyzing properties specific to each graph dataset.
    *   The computational cost of training DPMs can be high, and it would be beneficial to discuss the trade-offs between performance and computational efficiency.
    *   More ablation studies dissecting key component choices would allow for a richer understanding of design trade-offs.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning seems rather involved, as discussed in the paper. More detail on how these choices affect performance would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** Graffe's clear theoretical framework and strong empirical results are likely to inspire further research in the use of DPMs for graph representation learning. The Diff-InfoMax principle may become a widely adopted concept in the field. The framework also has potential applications in areas such as drug discovery, social network analysis, and recommendation systems.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

Graffe represents a significant advancement in the field of graph representation learning by successfully integrating diffusion probabilistic models. The theoretical grounding, strong empirical performance, and flexible framework contribute substantially to the field. While the paper could benefit from more detailed analysis of specific dataset characteristics, and discussion of computational costs, its overall impact is considerable. The integration of DPMs, a relatively recent approach, for graph representation learning, is both novel and likely to impact the community. I would have assigned a 9 if the weaknesses identified had been more thoroughly analyzed. A score of 8 accurately reflects the significant contributions and the remaining opportunities for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Learning Item Representations Directly from Multimodal Features for Effective Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04960v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Zhou, Xiaoxiong Zhang, Dusit Niyato, Zhiqi Shen
- **Abstract**: Conventional multimodal recommender systems predominantly leverage Bayesian Personalized Ranking (BPR) optimization to learn item representations by amalgamating item identity (ID) embeddings with multimodal features. Nevertheless, our empirical and theoretical findings unequivocally demonstrate a pronounced optimization gradient bias in favor of acquiring representations from multimodal features over item ID embeddings. As a consequence, item ID embeddings frequently exhibit suboptimal characteristics despite the convergence of multimodal feature parameters. Given the rich informational content inherent in multimodal features, in this paper, we propose a novel model (i.e., LIRDRec) that learns item representations directly from these features to augment recommendation performance. Recognizing that features derived from each modality may capture disparate yet correlated aspects of items, we propose a multimodal transformation mechanism, integrated with modality-specific encoders, to effectively fuse features from all modalities. Moreover, to differentiate the influence of diverse modality types, we devise a progressive weight copying fusion module within LIRDRec. This module incrementally learns the weight assigned to each modality in synthesizing the final user or item representations. Finally, we utilize the powerful visual understanding of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to convert the item images into texts and extract semantics embeddings upon the texts via LLMs. Empirical evaluations conducted on five real-world datasets validate the superiority of our approach relative to competing baselines. It is worth noting the proposed model, equipped with embeddings extracted from MLLMs and LLMs, can further improve the recommendation accuracy of NDCG@20 by an average of 4.21% compared to the original embeddings.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided research paper. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation and score.

**Paper Summary:**

The paper "Learning Item Representations Directly from Multimodal Features for Effective Recommendation" addresses a key limitation in conventional multimodal recommender systems (MMRSs): the optimization gradient bias towards multimodal features often overshadows the learning of item ID embeddings, leading to suboptimal performance.  The authors propose LIRDRec (Learns Item Representations Directly from Recommendation), a novel model that learns item representations *directly* from multimodal features (text and images) without relying on item ID embeddings.  LIRDRec utilizes a multimodal transformation mechanism (employing Discrete Cosine Transform and Deep Neural Networks) to effectively fuse features from various modalities, a progressive weight copying module to differentiate the influence of modalities, and leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) for semantic embedding extraction from item images and descriptions. The paper presents experimental results on five real-world datasets, demonstrating the superiority of LIRDRec over existing baselines, particularly in cold-start scenarios and when utilizing embeddings extracted from MLLMs and LLMs.  A theoretical analysis further supports the claim that gradient magnitude is skewed towards multimodal features during early training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strength:** The central idea of *completely* abandoning item ID embeddings in favor of directly learning item representations solely from multimodal features is a novel approach in the MMRS field.  Most existing methods treat multimodal information as supplementary to ID embeddings. The paper's theoretical justification and empirical evidence for this approach is well explained.
    *   **Weakness:** The individual components of LIRDRec (feature fusion with DNNs, attention mechanisms, graph convolutions) are not entirely novel in themselves.  However, the specific *combination* of these techniques, and the overarching philosophy of eliminating ID embeddings, constitutes a novel contribution.  The use of 2D DCT is an interesting choice for modality-specific feature extraction, but its novelty in this particular recommendation context is relatively minor.
*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   **Strength:** The potential impact of LIRDRec lies in its ability to improve recommendation performance, particularly in cold-start scenarios where ID embeddings are less effective. The reported improvements over strong baselines are statistically significant and highlight the potential of the direct learning approach.  The integration of MLLMs and LLMs for feature extraction shows a path to further performance gains and highlights the importance of high-quality multimodal features.
    *   **Weakness:** The experiments, while thorough, could be expanded to include more diverse datasets and a more thorough hyperparameter tuning analysis (although the main results are strong enough to support the model). Also, while the analysis shows a good improvement from the original embeddings to the ones generated via MLLMs/LLMs, a direct comparison with state-of-the-art models that solely rely on such models for recommendation could further strengthen the result.
*   **Technical Soundness:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper presents a clear theoretical analysis that supports the empirical findings. The methodology is well-defined and the experimental setup is reasonably rigorous, using standard evaluation metrics and datasets.
    *   **Weakness:**  The complexity of LIRDRec (with DCT, DNNs, GCNs, and PWC) might make it computationally expensive compared to simpler models. The paper lacks a detailed analysis of the computational complexity and efficiency of LIRDRec.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it relatively easy to understand the proposed methodology and the experimental results.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to multimodal recommendation that challenges the conventional wisdom of relying on item ID embeddings. While the individual components of LIRDRec are not entirely groundbreaking, their combination and the underlying principle of direct feature learning constitute a significant contribution. The empirical results demonstrate tangible performance improvements, especially in cold-start scenarios, and the integration of MLLMs and LLMs points to a promising direction for future research. Although the paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of computational complexity and an expansion of the experimental setup, the overall quality and potential impact warrant a high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### DenseGrounding: Improving Dense Language-Vision Semantics for Ego-Centric 3D Visual Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04965v1)
- **Authors**: Henry Zheng, Hao Shi, Qihang Peng, Yong Xien Chng, Rui Huang, Yepeng Weng, Zhongchao Shi, Gao Huang
- **Abstract**: Enabling intelligent agents to comprehend and interact with 3D environments through natural language is crucial for advancing robotics and human-computer interaction. A fundamental task in this field is ego-centric 3D visual grounding, where agents locate target objects in real-world 3D spaces based on verbal descriptions. However, this task faces two significant challenges: (1) loss of fine-grained visual semantics due to sparse fusion of point clouds with ego-centric multi-view images, (2) limited textual semantic context due to arbitrary language descriptions. We propose DenseGrounding, a novel approach designed to address these issues by enhancing both visual and textual semantics. For visual features, we introduce the Hierarchical Scene Semantic Enhancer, which retains dense semantics by capturing fine-grained global scene features and facilitating cross-modal alignment. For text descriptions, we propose a Language Semantic Enhancer that leverages large language models to provide rich context and diverse language descriptions with additional context during model training. Extensive experiments show that DenseGrounding significantly outperforms existing methods in overall accuracy, with improvements of 5.81% and 7.56% when trained on the comprehensive full dataset and smaller mini subset, respectively, further advancing the SOTA in egocentric 3D visual grounding. Our method also achieves 1st place and receives the Innovation Award in the CVPR 2024 Autonomous Grand Challenge Multi-view 3D Visual Grounding Track, validating its effectiveness and robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the "DenseGrounding: Improving Dense Language-Vision Semantics for Ego-Centric 3D Visual Grounding" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of ego-centric 3D visual grounding, specifically the loss of fine-grained visual semantics due to sparse point cloud fusion and limited contextual information in language descriptions. The authors propose "DenseGrounding," a method that enhances both visual and textual semantics.  It introduces a Hierarchical Scene Semantic Enhancer (HSSE) to retain dense visual semantics and a Language Semantic Enhancer (LSE) that leverages large language models (LLMs) to provide richer textual context for training. Experiments demonstrate improved accuracy compared to existing methods, and the method achieved first place in a CVPR 2024 challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** Ego-centric 3D visual grounding is an important task for embodied AI. The paper identifies and tackles key limitations of current approaches: sparsity of visual data and lack of contextual awareness in language.
    *   **Novelty in Approach:** The HSSE and LSE modules appear to be novel contributions that effectively address the identified problems. The HSSE's hierarchical approach to capturing scene-level semantics and the LSE's use of LLMs to enrich descriptions with scene context are well-motivated.  The semantic broadcast in HSSE to infuse the refined global scene-level semantics into the multi-scale depth features seems like a key element.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents solid experimental results, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods on the EmbodiedScan dataset. The ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of individual components. The first-place finish in a CVPR challenge lends further credence to the method's robustness and effectiveness. The performance gains on "hard" samples (scenes with multiple similar objects) are particularly compelling.
    *   **Well-written and structured:** The paper is well-written, clearly explaining the proposed method and the experimental setup. The figures and tables are helpful in understanding the approach and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Large Language Models (LLMs):**  The reliance on LLMs, while effective, introduces a dependence on external models. This raises concerns about computational cost, accessibility, and potential biases inherited from the LLM.  The specific LLM used (GPT4-o mini) and the process of prompt engineering significantly influence performance.
    *   **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While the paper demonstrates robustness through cross-dataset evaluation, it lacks a deep discussion of the limitations of zero-shot generalization. Further analysis of why the approach generalizes well and in which cases it might fail would be valuable.
    *   **Incremental Nature:** The work can be seen as an improvement over an existing SOTA method.  While the improvements are substantial, the core pipeline is borrowed from EmbodiedScan, reducing the overall novelty. The "enhanced baseline" further complicates the assessment of novelty, as it's not entirely clear how much of the improvement stems from the baseline enhancements versus the proposed modules.
    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:**  A more detailed error analysis, beyond the "easy" vs. "hard" categorization, could provide deeper insights into the failure cases of the method and guide future research.
    *   **Limited discussion on inference cost:** Although mentioned that for inference the model processes descriptions directly, without enhancement, it will be helpful to compare the inference time between the baseline.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper advances the state-of-the-art in ego-centric 3D visual grounding, a critical task for embodied AI.
    *   The proposed HSSE and LSE modules provide valuable insights into how to improve both visual and textual semantics in this domain.
    *   The work has the potential to influence future research directions in visual grounding and embodied AI.
    *   Securing the top spot and the Innovation Award in the CVPR Autonomous Grand Challenge Multi-view 3D Visual Grounding Track solidifies the value.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of ego-centric 3D visual grounding.  The performance improvements are significant and the proposed HSSE and LSE modules are well-motivated and effective. The empirical results are strong, and the paper is well-written. However, the reliance on LLMs, the incremental nature of the work, and the lack of discussion on failure cases limit the overall novelty and impact. While the improvements are notable and demonstrate a clear advance in performance, the underlying framework of EmbodiedScan means the novelty is primarily in the added modules, not a complete rethinking of the problem. Also, the dependence on LLM and the potential for bias should be addressed. Considering these factors, a score of 7 is appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReAlign: Bilingual Text-to-Motion Generation via Step-Aware Reward-Guided Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04974v1)
- **Authors**: Wanjiang Weng, Xiaofeng Tan, Hongsong Wang, Pan Zhou
- **Abstract**: Bilingual text-to-motion generation, which synthesizes 3D human motions from bilingual text inputs, holds immense potential for cross-linguistic applications in gaming, film, and robotics. However, this task faces critical challenges: the absence of bilingual motion-language datasets and the misalignment between text and motion distributions in diffusion models, leading to semantically inconsistent or low-quality motions. To address these challenges, we propose BiHumanML3D, a novel bilingual human motion dataset, which establishes a crucial benchmark for bilingual text-to-motion generation models. Furthermore, we propose a Bilingual Motion Diffusion model (BiMD), which leverages cross-lingual aligned representations to capture semantics, thereby achieving a unified bilingual model. Building upon this, we propose Reward-guided sampling Alignment (ReAlign) method, comprising a step-aware reward model to assess alignment quality during sampling and a reward-guided strategy that directs the diffusion process toward an optimally aligned distribution. This reward model integrates step-aware tokens and combines a text-aligned module for semantic consistency and a motion-aligned module for realism, refining noisy motions at each timestep to balance probability density and alignment. Experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves text-motion alignment and motion quality compared to existing state-of-the-art methods. Project page: https://wengwanjiang.github.io/ReAlign-page/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ReAlign: Bilingual Text-to-Motion Generation via Step-Aware Reward-Guided Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of bilingual text-to-motion generation, which aims to create 3D human motions from text descriptions in two languages. The authors identify two key challenges: the lack of bilingual motion-language datasets and the misalignment between text and motion distributions in diffusion models. To address these, they introduce BiHumanML3D, a new bilingual dataset; BiMD, a Bilingual Motion Diffusion model utilizing cross-lingual alignment; and ReAlign, a reward-guided sampling alignment method that incorporates a step-aware reward model to improve text-motion consistency and motion realism. The reward model combines a text-aligned module for semantic coherence and a motion-aligned module for realism, refining motions at each timestep to balance probability density and alignment. Experiments demonstrate that this approach improves both text-motion alignment and motion quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Bilingual Dataset:** Creating BiHumanML3D addresses a significant gap in the availability of bilingual motion-language data. This is crucial for advancing research in this area.
    *   **Bilingual Motion Diffusion Model (BiMD):**  The use of cross-lingual alignment to create a unified bilingual model is a valuable contribution, moving away from training separate language-specific models. The strategy leverages shared semantic information across languages.
    *   **Reward-Guided Alignment (ReAlign):** The step-aware reward model is innovative. Explicitly accounting for noise variations across diffusion timesteps and integrating both text-alignment and motion-realism into a reward signal is a significant improvement over previous methods that primarily focus on fine-tuning motion quality. The plug-and-play nature of ReAlign is also a strong positive.

*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to be influential for several reasons:
    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** Bilingual text-to-motion generation broadens accessibility and applicability of motion synthesis to a global audience.
    *   **Provides a benchmark:**  BiHumanML3D will likely become a valuable resource for the research community, facilitating further progress in bilingual motion generation.
    *   **Improves alignment:** The ReAlign method demonstrably enhances text-motion coherence, addressing a fundamental challenge in diffusion-based motion generation. The gains observed by plugging this approach to existing methods underscore this.
    *   **Cross-lingual learning:** The model contributes to the broader area of cross-lingual learning and generative modeling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-motivated, with clear explanations of the challenges involved.
    *   The proposed solutions are well-designed and technically sound.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive and provide strong evidence to support the claims made in the paper.
    *   The visual results (Figure 1 and S1) clearly show the improvements achieved by the proposed method.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component of the proposed framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** The model's reliance on pre-trained text encoders (OpenCLIP, XLM) and the diffusion model architecture is a point of consideration. Performance is intrinsically linked to the quality of these components. The dependency on a pre-trained diffusion model could limit how effectively the model samples *from* the generated space.
    *   **Generality of BiHumanML3D:** The paper does not deeply explore how well BiHumanML3D generalizes across different motion styles or complexities. The paper could benefit from exploring failure cases more fully.
    *   **Limited Language Scope:** While bilingual, the dataset only covers English and Chinese. Extending this to more languages would further increase its impact.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes significant contributions to the emerging field of bilingual text-to-motion generation. The introduction of the BiHumanML3D dataset fills a critical gap, while the BiMD model and ReAlign strategy represent valuable technical advances in cross-lingual semantic alignment and motion synthesis. The plug-and-play nature of ReAlign enhances its practical value. The limitations (dependency on pre-trained models and limited language scope) are acknowledged and do not diminish the core contributions. While further exploration of the limitations would bolster the paper, the strengths far outweigh the weaknesses. Therefore, a score of 8 reflects the paper's substantial novelty and potential for impact.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### ChainMarks: Securing DNN Watermark with Cryptographic Chain
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04977v1)
- **Authors**: Brian Choi, Shu Wang, Isabelle Choi, Kun Sun
- **Abstract**: With the widespread deployment of deep neural network (DNN) models, dynamic watermarking techniques are being used to protect the intellectual property of model owners. However, recent studies have shown that existing watermarking schemes are vulnerable to watermark removal and ambiguity attacks. Besides, the vague criteria for determining watermark presence further increase the likelihood of such attacks. In this paper, we propose a secure DNN watermarking scheme named ChainMarks, which generates secure and robust watermarks by introducing a cryptographic chain into the trigger inputs and utilizes a two-phase Monte Carlo method for determining watermark presence. First, ChainMarks generates trigger inputs as a watermark dataset by repeatedly applying a hash function over a secret key, where the target labels associated with trigger inputs are generated from the digital signature of model owner. Then, the watermarked model is produced by training a DNN over both the original and watermark datasets. To verify watermarks, we compare the predicted labels of trigger inputs with the target labels and determine ownership with a more accurate decision threshold that considers the classification probability of specific models. Experimental results show that ChainMarks exhibits higher levels of robustness and security compared to state-of-the-art watermarking schemes. With a better marginal utility, ChainMarks provides a higher probability guarantee of watermark presence in DNN models with the same level of watermark accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ChainMarks: Securing DNN Watermark with Cryptographic Chain":

**Summary:**

The paper "ChainMarks" proposes a novel dynamic DNN watermarking scheme designed to be more robust against watermark removal and ambiguity attacks than existing methods. The key idea is to introduce a cryptographic chain into the trigger inputs.  The trigger inputs are generated by repeatedly applying a hash function to a secret key. The target labels for these inputs are derived from the digital signature of the model owner. Watermark verification involves checking if the predicted labels for the generated trigger inputs match the target labels, using a two-phase Monte Carlo method to determine a decision threshold that accounts for the classification probabilities of the DNN. The paper claims improved robustness and security compared to existing methods, with better marginal utility (i.e., higher probability guarantee of watermark presence for a given level of accuracy).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The introduction of a cryptographic chain to secure trigger inputs is a genuinely novel contribution. This approach effectively counters watermark ambiguity attacks that plague many existing methods. Using a two-phase Monte Carlo method to estimate the watermark presence threshold also presents a significant improvement over basic empirical methods, particularly in situations with small *p*-values.

*   **Significance:** Securing DNN models' intellectual property remains a crucial challenge as these models become more prevalent. The vulnerabilities of existing watermarking schemes necessitate more robust solutions. ChainMarks provides a significant step in this direction. The robustness and security against attacks are promising and address key weaknesses in current methodologies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Robustness against Ambiguity Attacks:** The primary strength is the cryptographic chain that effectively prevents the optimization-based attacks needed to forge alternative watermarks.
    *   **Robustness against Removal Attacks:**  The use of "noise-like" trigger inputs which are far from the real input distributions helps to defend from attacks like fine-tuning.
    *   **Improved Threshold Estimation:** The two-phase Monte Carlo technique provides a more accurate means of deciding on watermark presence, particularly when low *p*-values are important for high security.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experiments, comparing ChainMarks with four other watermarking schemes, against a range of attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Assumptions about Access to Datasets:** The paper assumes the model owner has access to the original training dataset. This might not always be practical, especially when outsourcing model training.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** Although the core concept is promising, the application to different DNN architectures (other than ResNets) or input data types (text, graphs) requires further investigation. The discussion of scalability to larger datasets (ImageNet) offers only high-level guidance.
    *  **Potential for Scalability Issues for Longer Chains:** While cryptographic chains offer a robust defence mechanism, the practical implementation of very long chains can face scalability issues, since they might necessitate a larger memory footprint for both embedding and verification.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the design of future DNN watermarking schemes. The introduction of cryptographic principles offers a new direction in securing watermarks, and the careful consideration of attack scenarios is valuable. The two-phase Monte Carlo estimation could become a standard technique for decision threshold calculations. It lays the ground work for future research on further integrating cryptographic techniques to increase security of the DNN models.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the identified strengths and weaknesses, a score of 8 is appropriate. The novelty of the cryptographic chain provides a significant advancement in defending against watermark ambiguity attacks, a crucial problem in the field. The evaluation is comprehensive, demonstrating ChainMarks' superior performance. However, the assumptions about dataset access and limited generalizability (only images tested), along with potential scalability issues, prevent a higher score.
The paper has the potential to stimulate considerable follow-up research in more robust and secure DNN watermarking techniques.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Latent Preference Coding: Aligning Large Language Models via Discrete Latent Codes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04993v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuocheng Gong, Jian Guan, Wei Wu, Huishuai Zhang, Dongyan Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success, yet aligning their generations with human preferences remains a critical challenge. Existing approaches to preference modeling often rely on an explicit or implicit reward function, overlooking the intricate and multifaceted nature of human preferences that may encompass conflicting factors across diverse tasks and populations. To address this limitation, we introduce Latent Preference Coding (LPC), a novel framework that models the implicit factors as well as their combinations behind holistic preferences using discrete latent codes. LPC seamlessly integrates with various offline alignment algorithms, automatically inferring the underlying factors and their importance from data without relying on pre-defined reward functions and hand-crafted combination weights. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that LPC consistently improves upon three alignment algorithms (DPO, SimPO, and IPO) using three base models (Mistral-7B, Llama3-8B, and Llama3-8B-Instruct). Furthermore, deeper analysis reveals that the learned latent codes effectively capture the differences in the distribution of human preferences and significantly enhance the robustness of alignment against noise in data. By providing a unified representation for the multifarious preference factors, LPC paves the way towards developing more robust and versatile alignment techniques for the responsible deployment of powerful LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latent Preference Coding (LPC), a novel framework for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. LPC addresses the limitations of existing methods that often rely on a single reward function and fail to capture the multifaceted nature of human preferences. The key idea is to model implicit preference factors as discrete latent codes using variational inference. This allows the model to automatically infer the underlying factors and their importance from data without predefined reward functions or handcrafted weights. The LPC framework is designed to be compatible with various offline alignment algorithms such as DPO, SimPO and IPO. The experiments on multiple benchmarks using Mistral-7B and Llama3-8B demonstrates that LPC can improve performance over existing alignment algorithms. Additional analysis reveals that LPC captures differences in human preference distributions and shows robustness to noisy data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in modeling the complex, multifaceted nature of human preferences with discrete latent codes learned through variational inference. It's a departure from traditional single-reward function approaches. The formulation for combining this with existing offline RLHF algorithms also contributes to the novelty. While latent variable models aren't entirely new, their application in this specific context of LLM alignment, with a focus on interpretability and integration with existing algorithms, is innovative. The paper distinguishes itself from concurrent works employing latent variables by using *discrete* latent variables, which offer better interpretability.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to enhance the robustness, versatility, and responsible deployment of LLMs. The framework addresses a key challenge in LLM alignment: the difficulty of accurately capturing complex human preferences. The ability to model preference factors without relying on explicit reward functions has the potential to simplify the alignment process and reduce the need for extensive human annotation. The demonstrated improvement in robustness against noisy data is also significant for real-world applications. By providing a unified representation, LPC might facilitate the development of more generalizable alignment techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the challenges in preference modeling for LLMs.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The proposed LPC framework is a novel and well-motivated approach to address these challenges.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive and cover a wide range of benchmarks, base models, and alignment algorithms.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the effectiveness of different components of the LPC framework. The analysis of latent codes is also insightful.
    *   **Open-Source Library:** The paper mentions their use of the OpenRLHF library and contributions, which are great for reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **MMLU Results:** The performance on MMLU doesn't show significant improvements with LPC. Though the authors explain it with the domain gap and the nature of the benchmark, this does highlight a limitation in the generalizability of LPC.
    *   **Complexity:** The formulation of LPC is relatively complex, although the authors argue that the implementation is simple. The complexity might be a barrier to adoption by some researchers. The reliance on variational inference requires careful tuning to avoid issues such as posterior collapse, even with discrete latent variables.
    *   **Limited Experiments with More Advanced Models:** The experiments are performed primarily with Mistral-7B and Llama3-8B. Evaluating LPC with more advanced and larger LLMs would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Human Preferences in the Loop:** The paper argues about learning human preferences, however, there is not human evaluation and no effort to extend LPC to account for population differences, which might be something to explore.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a novel contribution to the field of LLM alignment by introducing a framework that effectively models complex human preferences using discrete latent codes. It addresses a critical limitation of existing methods and offers a more robust and versatile approach. The comprehensive experimental evaluation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the LPC framework. While there are some weaknesses, such as the complexity and limited generalizability, the paper's strengths outweigh its limitations. The potential for enabling more responsible and effective deployment of powerful LLMs is significant. The paper is well-written and clearly presents its ideas.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking Invariance in In-context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04994v1)
- **Authors**: Lizhe Fang, Yifei Wang, Khashayar Gatmiry, Lei Fang, Yisen Wang
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) has emerged as a pivotal capability of auto-regressive large language models, yet it is hindered by a notable sensitivity to the ordering of context examples regardless of their mutual independence. To address this issue, recent studies have introduced several variant algorithms of ICL that achieve permutation invariance. However, many of these do not exhibit comparable performance with the standard auto-regressive ICL algorithm. In this work, we identify two crucial elements in the design of an invariant ICL algorithm: information non-leakage and context interdependence, which are not simultaneously achieved by any of the existing methods. These investigations lead us to the proposed Invariant ICL (InvICL), a methodology designed to achieve invariance in ICL while ensuring the two properties. Empirically, our findings reveal that InvICL surpasses previous models, both invariant and non-invariant, in most benchmark datasets, showcasing superior generalization capabilities across varying input lengths. Code is available at https://github.com/PKU-ML/InvICL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the order sensitivity issue in In-Context Learning (ICL), where the performance of large language models is affected by the order of the context examples even when they are independent. It argues that existing methods aiming for permutation invariance often compromise other important properties like information non-leakage and context interdependence. The authors propose Invariant ICL (InvICL), a method that leverages leave-one-out embeddings to achieve invariance while preserving these properties. InvICL is designed to prevent queries from accessing their answers, thereby avoiding trivial solutions, while enhancing context interaction by allowing each example to interact with all others.  The paper shows empirically that InvICL outperforms existing ICL methods, including both invariant and non-invariant ones, on several benchmarks, demonstrating better generalization and performance across varying input lengths.  A parallel implementation is also introduced to address computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using leave-one-out embeddings to simultaneously achieve permutation invariance, information non-leakage, and context interdependence in ICL has moderate novelty.  While techniques like leave-one-out are not entirely new, their specific application to address the identified shortcomings of existing invariant ICL methods is a valuable contribution. The decomposition of the desiderata for invariant ICL into information non-leakage and context interdependence is itself a useful contribution, clarifying design choices in the field.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it tackles a well-known problem (order sensitivity in ICL) with a principled approach. By identifying and addressing the limitations of previous attempts at permutation invariance, the authors provide a more robust and effective solution. The empirical results, showing consistent improvements over existing methods in various benchmarks including OOD setting and different models, suggest that InvICL has practical value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear Problem Definition: The paper clearly articulates the issue of order sensitivity and the trade-offs involved in achieving permutation invariance.
    *   Principled Design: The InvICL method is based on sound reasoning, carefully considering the desired properties and their impact on performance. The two stage design is innovative and impactful.
    *   Strong Empirical Results: The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of InvICL, showing improvements over various baselines on both synthetic and real-world datasets. Length generalization performance is also notable.
    *   Parallel implementation further enhanced the practical significance of this paper.
    *   The inclusion of the theoretical discussion and linear probing helps further understanding the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the theoretical analysis offers insights, a deeper theoretical understanding of why InvICL performs so well across different tasks and how it truly alleviates order sensitivity would further strengthen the paper.
    *   The parallel implementation alleviates computational cost, but the memory overhead could be a limiting factor for very long sequences. A discussion of memory constraints and potential solutions would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in ICL by highlighting the importance of information non-leakage and context interdependence when designing invariant methods. The InvICL method itself could serve as a foundation for further improvements and extensions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of ICL. It offers a well-reasoned and empirically validated solution to a significant problem, while also providing valuable insights into the design of invariant ICL methods. The weaknesses identified are not major and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. However, a higher score requires even more convincing theoretical contribution. Therefore, I would give it a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.04996v1)
- **Authors**: Jinhe Huang, Yongkang Cheng, Yuming Hang, Gaoge Han, Jinewei Li, Jing Zhang, Xingjian Gu
- **Abstract**: Full-body gestures play a pivotal role in natural interactions and are crucial for achieving effective communication. Nevertheless, most existing studies primarily focus on the gesture generation of speakers, overlooking the vital role of listeners in the interaction process and failing to fully explore the dynamic interaction between them. This paper innovatively proposes an Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication. For the first time, we integrate the full-body gestures of listeners into the generation framework. By devising a novel inter-diffusion mechanism, this model can accurately capture the complex interaction patterns between speakers and listeners during communication. In the model construction process, based on the advanced diffusion model architecture, we innovatively introduce interaction conditions and the GAN model to increase the denoising step size. As a result, when generating gesture sequences, the model can not only dynamically generate based on the speaker's speech information but also respond in realtime to the listener's feedback, enabling synergistic interaction between the two. Abundant experimental results demonstrate that compared with the current state-of-the-art gesture generation methods, the model we proposed has achieved remarkable improvements in the naturalness, coherence, and speech-gesture synchronization of the generated gestures. In the subjective evaluation experiments, users highly praised the generated interaction scenarios, believing that they are closer to real life human communication situations. Objective index evaluations also show that our model outperforms the baseline methods in multiple key indicators, providing more powerful support for effective communication.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an "Inter-Diffusion Generation Model of Speakers and Listeners for Effective Communication." The core idea is to extend existing gesture generation models, which primarily focus on speakers, to include the gestures of listeners within a conversational setting. The authors argue that listener gestures are crucial for realistic and effective communication and that existing models fail to capture this dynamic interaction. They propose an inter-diffusion mechanism, using a diffusion model architecture with interaction conditions and a GAN, to generate synchronized speaker and listener gestures based on speech input and real-time feedback.  The model aims to capture the complex interaction patterns between speakers and listeners. The paper presents experimental results that claim improved naturalness, coherence, and speech-gesture synchronization compared to state-of-the-art methods. User studies are also conducted to subjectively evaluate the generated interactions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its attempt to model *listener* gestures in addition to speaker gestures within a co-speech gesture generation framework.  Most prior work concentrates solely on the speaker. This is a worthwhile endeavor, as it addresses a clear gap in the existing literature. The proposed "Inter-Diffusion" mechanism, intended to capture the dynamic interaction between speakers and listeners, is presented as a new architectural component.  However, the technical details suggest that this involves adapting and combining existing techniques (diffusion models, GANs, attention mechanisms) rather than introducing a truly revolutionary concept. The use of speaker/listener role-specific condition weighting during the diffusion process also contributes to novelty.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is potentially high.  If the proposed model can effectively generate realistic and contextually appropriate listener gestures, it could significantly improve the realism of virtual communication scenarios.  Applications in VR/AR, gaming, and film could benefit from more natural and engaging interactions. However, the impact hinges on the *quality* of the generated gestures and how realistically the model captures listener behavior.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a gap:** The paper directly tackles a neglected area in gesture generation.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The motivation and shortcomings of existing methods are clearly articulated.
*   **Use of appropriate datasets:** The use of TWH and ZeroEGG to build a combined dataset including listener gestures is a reasonable approach.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes both objective metrics (FGD, BA, DIV) and a user study, which is important for evaluating the subjective quality of generated gestures.
*   **Ablation studies**: Ablation studies show the effectiveness of various modules on the model.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Innovation:** While the idea is compelling, the technical innovation seems incremental. The "Inter-Diffusion" mechanism appears to be a combination and adaptation of existing techniques. More in-depth explanations and theoretical justification for choices made would strengthen the work.
*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper provides a high-level overview of the model architecture but lacks detailed explanations of the specific implementation choices and hyperparameter settings.
*   **Dataset limitations**: There might be significant issues with the method for merging information from different datasets and ensuring synchronization of the data, which can have major impact on the final performance.
*   **User study limitations**: the number of users who were involved in the study isn't large, which can lead to bias and reduced statistical significance.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign the paper a score of **6**.

Here's why:

*   The core *idea* of modeling listener gestures is valuable and addresses a real weakness in existing gesture generation research. This alone pushes it above a purely incremental work.
*   The technical *implementation*, while not revolutionary, demonstrates an ability to adapt existing techniques in a novel way.
*   However, the limited technical depth and the impression that the Inter-Diffusion framework is more of an *integration* than a groundbreaking architectural leap prevent a higher score. The GAN component especially lacks a thorough explanation.
*   The experimental results, while positive, require further scrutiny and more rigorous analysis of the datasets used.
* Overall, it does add enough novel work to warrant contribution to the field.

In summary, the paper makes a solid contribution by tackling a neglected problem in gesture generation. However, the technical innovation is somewhat incremental, and the paper could benefit from more detailed explanations and a stronger justification of design choices.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Pitfalls of Growing Group Complexity: LLMs and Social Choice-Based Aggregation for Group Recommendations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05016v1)
- **Authors**: Cedric Waterschoot, Nava Tintarev, Francesco Barile
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in recommender systems aimed at both individuals and groups. Previously, Group Recommender Systems (GRS) often used social choice-based aggregation strategies to derive a single recommendation based on the preferences of multiple people. In this paper, we investigate under which conditions language models can perform these strategies correctly based on zero-shot learning and analyse whether the formatting of the group scenario in the prompt affects accuracy. We specifically focused on the impact of group complexity (number of users and items), different LLMs, different prompting conditions, including In-Context learning or generating explanations, and the formatting of group preferences. Our results show that performance starts to deteriorate when considering more than 100 ratings. However, not all language models were equally sensitive to growing group complexity. Additionally, we showed that In-Context Learning (ICL) can significantly increase the performance at higher degrees of group complexity, while adding other prompt modifications, specifying domain cues or prompting for explanations, did not impact accuracy. We conclude that future research should include group complexity as a factor in GRS evaluation due to its effect on LLM performance. Furthermore, we showed that formatting the group scenarios differently, such as rating lists per user or per item, affected accuracy. All in all, our study implies that smaller LLMs are capable of generating group recommendations under the right conditions, making the case for using smaller models that require less computing power and costs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in group recommendation systems when applying social choice-based aggregation strategies. It focuses on the impact of "group complexity" (number of users and items) on the accuracy of these strategies, examining different LLMs, prompting conditions (including in-context learning and explanation generation), and data formatting methods. The study finds that performance degrades as group complexity increases, that in-context learning significantly improves performance at higher complexity, and that data formatting impacts accuracy. It highlights the need to consider group complexity during GRS evaluation with LLMs and advocates for using smaller models where possible.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and relatively underexplored issue: the impact of group complexity on LLM-based group recommendations, especially related to the use of social-choice based aggregation.  Prior works have often used LLMs directly as preference aggregators without fully considering the effect of input size/complexity on their ability to follow specific, implementable aggregation strategies (i.e., algorithms) . The exploration of in-context learning to mitigate this effect is also a positive aspect. The exploration of data formatting and how it impacts accuracy is a useful, although incremental, contribution.

**Significance:** This work has the potential to influence how LLMs are evaluated for group recommendation tasks. The finding that complexity matters suggests that future evaluations should include scenarios with varying group sizes and item numbers. The finding that smaller LLMs can, under the right conditions (prompt engineering, in-context learning), be used as aggregation methods is also significant for resource-constrained or privacy-aware applications. Highlighting the impact of seemingly minor input data format changes can improve replicability and transparency.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper articulates well-defined research questions.
*   **Comprehensive Experimental Design:** The study examines several relevant factors, including different LLMs, prompting strategies, and data formatting.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The findings offer actionable guidance for practitioners on how to evaluate and use LLMs in group recommendation systems.
*   **Reproducibility:** The availability of code and prompts enhances the reproducibility of the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Strategies:** While the paper includes examples from each category (consensus, majority, borderline), a broader set of social choice strategies could provide a more complete picture.
*   **Soft Accuracy Metric:** The use of an overlap-based accuracy metric is somewhat lenient, particularly considering that social choice strategies often result in a single winning item (or list of tied items). It might inflate accuracy somewhat by rewarding over-recommending. The paper does mention this and addresses potential risks.
*   **Dataset Generation:** The use of fictitious groups, while acceptable, limits the generalizability of the findings to real-world scenarios. It can be argued that it would have been more powerful to test on existing datasets.
*   **Limited Analysis of Natural Language Explanations:** The paper only examines the impact of generating explanations on accuracy and does not assess the quality or usefulness of the generated explanations themselves. More elaborate analysis of the generated explanations could strengthen the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a valuable contribution to the growing field of LLMs in recommender systems. Its novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of how group complexity affects the performance of LLMs when attempting to implement defined social-choice aggregation methods. While there are limitations, the study's clear findings and practical implications warrant a positive assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalable Multi-Stage Influence Function for Large Language Models via Eigenvalue-Corrected Kronecker-Factored Parameterization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05017v1)
- **Authors**: Yuntai Bao, Xuhong Zhang, Tianyu Du, Xinkui Zhao, Jiang Zong, Hao Peng, Jianwei Yin
- **Abstract**: Pre-trained large language models (LLMs) are commonly fine-tuned to adapt to downstream tasks. Since the majority of knowledge is acquired during pre-training, attributing the predictions of fine-tuned LLMs to their pre-training data may provide valuable insights. Influence functions have been proposed as a means to explain model predictions based on training data. However, existing approaches fail to compute ``multi-stage'' influence and lack scalability to billion-scale LLMs. In this paper, we propose the multi-stage influence function to attribute the downstream predictions of fine-tuned LLMs to pre-training data under the full-parameter fine-tuning paradigm. To enhance the efficiency and practicality of our multi-stage influence function, we leverage Eigenvalue-corrected Kronecker-Factored (EK-FAC) parameterization for efficient approximation. Empirical results validate the superior scalability of EK-FAC approximation and the effectiveness of our multi-stage influence function. Additionally, case studies on a real-world LLM, dolly-v2-3b, demonstrate its interpretive power, with exemplars illustrating insights provided by multi-stage influence estimates. Our code is public at https://github.com/colored-dye/multi_stage_influence_function.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of attributing the predictions of fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) to their pre-training data. The authors propose a multi-stage influence function (IF) that accounts for the two-stage (pre-train then fine-tune) training process.  To make the computation tractable for billion-scale models, they leverage Eigenvalue-corrected Kronecker-Factored (EK-FAC) parameterization for efficient approximation of the Hessian inverse. They also use semantic similarity heuristics to reduce the number of candidate training samples considered.  The paper presents experiments demonstrating the scalability of EK-FAC, the effectiveness of the multi-stage IF, and a case study on a real-world LLM (dolly-v2-3b) to show its interpretive power. The study finds that MLP parameters have a proportionally greater influence than MHA parameters, suggesting a practical trade-off.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper makes a worthwhile contribution by addressing a practical problem. While influence functions are not new, applying them to multi-stage LLM training scenarios is not a trivial extension, particularly with the goal of scalability. The combination of the multi-stage IF formulation with EK-FAC parameterization and semantic similarity-based candidate selection is a relatively novel combination.  The finding about the relative influence of MLP vs. MHA parameters is an interesting, albeit perhaps preliminary, empirical observation.

* **Significance:** The ability to trace the origins of LLM predictions back to their training data is highly relevant for several reasons:
    * **Interpretability:** It improves our understanding of how LLMs learn and what knowledge they acquire.
    * **Trustworthiness:** It allows users to assess the grounding of LLM responses and identify potential sources of bias or misinformation.
    * **Debugging:**  It facilitates the identification and mitigation of problematic training data.

The paper takes a solid step towards more interpretable and trustworthy LLMs. The experimental results, while not groundbreaking, provide empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The dolly-v2-3b case study illustrates the method's potential for providing valuable insights.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Practical Focus:** The paper tackles a real-world problem with a focus on scalability.
    * **Sound Methodology:**  The combination of the multi-stage IF, EK-FAC, and semantic similarity selection is well-motivated.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness and efficiency of the approach.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** The work builds on existing influence function methods and EK-FAC approximations, so the novelty is somewhat limited.
    * **Scope of Experiments:**  While the experiments cover several aspects of the approach, the fact-tracing benchmark may not fully capture the complexities of real-world LLM applications. A larger variety of tasks could further strengthen the results. Also, the fact tracing experiment has less improvement in recall@10.
    * **Generality of findings:** Finding about the relative influence of MLP vs MHA may be architecture specific and not generalize well to other language models.
    * **Limited comparison:** The paper did not conduct a direct comparison with the SOURCE method from Bae et al. [2024] due to lack of its public implementation.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more interpretable and trustworthy LLMs. The proposed approach could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to understand the behavior of their models and identify potential issues. The code is publicly available which facilitates its adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.  While the paper makes a significant contribution to the field of LLM interpretability, its novelty is somewhat incremental, as it builds on existing techniques. The practical focus and empirical validation are strong points, but the limited variety of experiments and direct comparisons limit the overall impact. There is also some scope of improvement, given that the proposed approach does not address concerns regarding other components such as embeddings. The presented method has a good, practical engineering approach to resolve a practical and important research problem. This paper will likely serve as a useful starting point for future work in this area.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SOAP: Style-Omniscient Animatable Portraits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05022v1)
- **Authors**: Tingting Liao, Yujian Zheng, Adilbek Karmanov, Liwen Hu, Leyang Jin, Yuliang Xiu, Hao Li
- **Abstract**: Creating animatable 3D avatars from a single image remains challenging due to style limitations (realistic, cartoon, anime) and difficulties in handling accessories or hairstyles. While 3D diffusion models advance single-view reconstruction for general objects, outputs often lack animation controls or suffer from artifacts because of the domain gap. We propose SOAP, a style-omniscient framework to generate rigged, topology-consistent avatars from any portrait. Our method leverages a multiview diffusion model trained on 24K 3D heads with multiple styles and an adaptive optimization pipeline to deform the FLAME mesh while maintaining topology and rigging via differentiable rendering. The resulting textured avatars support FACS-based animation, integrate with eyeballs and teeth, and preserve details like braided hair or accessories. Extensive experiments demonstrate the superiority of our method over state-of-the-art techniques for both single-view head modeling and diffusion-based generation of Image-to-3D. Our code and data are publicly available for research purposes at https://github.com/TingtingLiao/soap.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SOAP: Style-Omniscient Animatable Portraits":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SOAP, a novel framework for creating animatable 3D avatars from a single portrait image, regardless of the style (realistic, cartoon, anime, etc.). SOAP addresses the limitations of existing methods that are often style-specific or struggle with complex hairstyles and accessories. The method combines a multi-view diffusion model, trained on a large dataset of 3D heads with varying styles, with an adaptive optimization pipeline. This pipeline deforms a FLAME mesh to fit the input image, while preserving topology and rigging, thus enabling FACS-based animation, integration of eyeballs and teeth, and detailed representation of hairstyles and accessories.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *combination* of several techniques to achieve style-agnostic animatable 3D head reconstruction. While individual components like multi-view diffusion models and parametric head models exist, the integration is unique. The adaptive deformation and remeshing process to reconcile the output of the style-omniscient diffusion model with an animatable rig appear particularly novel. It is also the first that is truly style omniscient by design, learning and generalizing over the two extreme styles of human realism and non-realistic anime.
*   **Significance:** The ability to generate animatable avatars from a single image, across a wide range of styles, has significant implications for various applications like gaming, virtual reality, personalized avatars, and content creation. SOAP significantly lowers the barrier to entry for 3D avatar creation and opens up new possibilities for customization. Further, the focus on FACS-based animation and mesh-based output makes it compatible with existing animation pipelines, unlike many NeRF-based approaches. The release of the 24K 3D head dataset also represents a valuable contribution to the research community.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Style-agnostic Reconstruction:**  The framework demonstrably handles a broad range of styles, including realistic, cartoon, and anime, overcoming the limitations of style-specific approaches.
    *   **Animatability:**  The resulting avatars are fully animatable, with FACS-based animation, eye movements, and lip sync, making them useful for interactive applications.
    *   **Detailed Geometry:** SOAP captures complex hairstyles and accessories with good fidelity, addressing a common problem in single-view reconstruction.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:**  The publicly released dataset of 24k 3D heads is a significant resource for future research in the area.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on External Tools:** The framework relies on external tools for FLAME estimation, landmark detection, and head parsing, which may limit its robustness and performance on highly stylized inputs, as shown in the limitations section. It might suffer on inputs where these tools are unable to generate reliable initializations.
    *   **Resolution Limits:** The output resolution is limited by the diffusion model, potentially affecting the quality of fine details.  Increasing the output resolution could improve fidelity.
    *   **Failure Cases:** As with many ML based reconstruction techniques, SOAP has failure cases, resulting from wrong landmark initialization, errors in head parsing or when the initial FLAME estimation fails.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in single-view 3D head reconstruction and avatar generation. The style-agnostic approach and focus on animatability are valuable contributions. The combination of diffusion models with parametric head models and adaptive optimization could inspire new methods for combining generative and analytical techniques. The provided dataset will also prove to be quite important in the coming future.

Overall, the paper represents a significant advancement in single-view animatable 3D head reconstruction. The style-agnostic nature, detailed geometry capture, and full animatability are valuable contributions that address limitations of previous works. While the dependency on external tools and failure cases are present, the benefits outweigh the negatives.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:** SOAP combines multiple known techniques, specifically diffusion models and parametric models with differentiable rendering, to achieve a novel solution to the problem of style omniscient 3D reconstruction. The adaptive deformation process is particularly interesting and contributes to this. Also, by releasing a dataset of 24k samples, the authors enable further progress to be made in the field. However, since it builds on previous work significantly and isn't fundamentally groundbreaking, it is not a 9.0 or above.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### LSRP: A Leader-Subordinate Retrieval Framework for Privacy-Preserving Cloud-Device Collaboration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05031v1)
- **Authors**: Yingyi Zhang, Pengyue Jia, Xianneng Li, Derong Xu, Maolin Wang, Yichao Wang, Zhaocheng Du, Huifeng Guo, Yong Liu, Ruiming Tang, Xiangyu Zhao
- **Abstract**: Cloud-device collaboration leverages on-cloud Large Language Models (LLMs) for handling public user queries and on-device Small Language Models (SLMs) for processing private user data, collectively forming a powerful and privacy-preserving solution. However, existing approaches often fail to fully leverage the scalable problem-solving capabilities of on-cloud LLMs while underutilizing the advantage of on-device SLMs in accessing and processing personalized data. This leads to two interconnected issues: 1) Limited utilization of the problem-solving capabilities of on-cloud LLMs, which fail to align with personalized user-task needs, and 2) Inadequate integration of user data into on-device SLM responses, resulting in mismatches in contextual user information. In this paper, we propose a Leader-Subordinate Retrieval framework for Privacy-preserving cloud-device collaboration (LSRP), a novel solution that bridges these gaps by: 1) enhancing on-cloud LLM guidance to on-device SLM through a dynamic selection of task-specific leader strategies named as user-to-user retrieval-augmented generation (U-U-RAG), and 2) integrating the data advantages of on-device SLMs through small model feedback Direct Preference Optimization (SMFB-DPO) for aligning the on-cloud LLM with the on-device SLM. Experiments on two datasets demonstrate that LSRP consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, significantly improving question-answer relevance and personalization, while preserving user privacy through efficient on-device retrieval. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Zhang-Yingyi/LSRP.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the LSRP paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "LSRP: A Leader-Subordinate Retrieval Framework for Privacy-Preserving Cloud-Device Collaboration" addresses the challenges of combining the strengths of on-cloud Large Language Models (LLMs) with on-device Small Language Models (SLMs) while preserving user privacy. Existing cloud-device collaboration frameworks often fail to fully utilize both the problem-solving capabilities of LLMs and the data access advantages of SLMs. The authors propose LSRP, a framework that uses a "leader-subordinate" approach, with the on-cloud LLM guiding the on-device SLM. LSRP incorporates two key components: 1) User-to-User Retrieval Augmented Generation (U-U-RAG) to dynamically select task-specific leader strategies for the on-cloud LLM, and 2) a small model feedback-driven Direct Preference Optimization (SMFB-DPO) to align the on-cloud LLM with the on-device SLM based on user preferences and privacy. Experimental results on two datasets (CoGenisis and a newly created Movie Explain dataset) demonstrate that LSRP outperforms several baseline methods in terms of question-answer relevance and personalization.  The code is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach to cloud-device collaboration for privacy-preserving tasks. The combination of U-U-RAG for leader strategy selection and SMFB-DPO for on-cloud LLM alignment is a unique contribution. The idea of framing the on-cloud LLM as a "leader" providing guidance to the on-device "subordinate" SLM using Path-Goal Theory is interesting.
*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is highly relevant in the current landscape, with increasing concerns about user privacy and the desire to leverage powerful LLMs on resource-constrained devices. Demonstrating improved performance in relevance and personalization while preserving privacy is a valuable contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed framework and its components.
    *   The U-U-RAG and SMFB-DPO components are well-motivated and address specific limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements over several baseline methods.
    *   The inclusion of ablation studies provides insights into the contribution of each component.
    * The availability of code enhances reproducibility and impact.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a new synthetic dataset (Movie Explain) is a potential limitation. While the authors explain the data generation process, it's not clear how well it generalizes to real-world scenarios, and there is risk of dataset bias.
    *   While the CoGenisis dataset is open-source, information is needed to analyze it, and the quality should be guaranteed.
    *   The evaluation metrics, while standard, rely on LLM-as-a-judge. It would be stronger with human evaluations to validate the automated scores, even for a subset of the results.
    *   The improvements in Readability (FRE) are also worth noting.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the framework proves robust in more diverse and realistic settings, it could significantly impact how cloud-device collaboration is approached, particularly for applications requiring strong privacy guarantees.
*   **Justification for Score:** The paper is a valuable contribution to the field. It presents a novel and well-motivated framework with strong experimental results. However, some weaknesses limit the overall impact and scope of the work. Therefore, the score reflects a strong positive, but not exceptional, contribution.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Divide-and-Conquer: Cold-Start Bundle Recommendation via Mixture of Diffusion Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05035v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Li, Lin Li, Xiaohui Tao, Dong Zhang, Jimmy Xiangji Huang
- **Abstract**: Cold-start bundle recommendation focuses on modeling new bundles with insufficient information to provide recommendations. Advanced bundle recommendation models usually learn bundle representations from multiple views (e.g., interaction view) at both the bundle and item levels. Consequently, the cold-start problem for bundles is more challenging than that for traditional items due to the dual-level multi-view complexity. In this paper, we propose a novel Mixture of Diffusion Experts (MoDiffE) framework, which employs a divide-and-conquer strategy for cold-start bundle recommendation and follows three steps:(1) Divide: The bundle cold-start problem is divided into independent but similar sub-problems sequentially by level and view, which can be summarized as the poor representation of feature-missing bundles in prior-embedding models. (2) Conquer: Beyond prior-embedding models that fundamentally provide the embedded representations, we introduce a diffusion-based method to solve all sub-problems in a unified way, which directly generates diffusion representations using diffusion models without depending on specific features. (3) Combine: A cold-aware hierarchical Mixture of Experts (MoE) is employed to combine results of the sub-problems for final recommendations, where the two models for each view serve as experts and are adaptively fused for different bundles in a multi-layer manner. Additionally, MoDiffE adopts a multi-stage decoupled training pipeline and introduces a cold-start gating augmentation method to enable the training of gating for cold bundles. Through extensive experiments on three real-world datasets, we demonstrate that MoDiffE significantly outperforms existing solutions in handling cold-start bundle recommendation. It achieves up to a 0.1027 absolute gain in Recall@20 in cold-start scenarios and up to a 47.43\% relative improvement in all-bundle scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MoDiffE (Mixture of Diffusion Experts), a novel framework for cold-start bundle recommendation. It tackles the challenge of recommending new bundles with limited interaction data by employing a divide-and-conquer strategy.  MoDiffE divides the cold-start problem into independent sub-problems at both bundle and item levels and across different views (e.g., interaction, content).
