- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05225v1)
- **Authors**: Mengze Hong, Wailing Ng, Di Jiang, Chen Jason Zhang
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of Chinese large language models (LLMs) underscores the need for domain-specific evaluations to ensure reliable applications. However, existing benchmarks often lack coverage in vertical domains and offer limited insights into the Chinese working context. Leveraging qualification exams as a unified framework for human expertise evaluation, we introduce QualBench, the first multi-domain Chinese QA benchmark dedicated to localized assessment of Chinese LLMs. The dataset includes over 17,000 questions across six vertical domains, with data selections grounded in 24 Chinese qualifications to closely align with national policies and working standards. Through comprehensive evaluation, the Qwen2.5 model outperformed the more advanced GPT-4o, with Chinese LLMs consistently surpassing non-Chinese models, highlighting the importance of localized domain knowledge in meeting qualification requirements. The best performance of 75.26% reveals the current gaps in domain coverage within model capabilities. Furthermore, we present the failure of LLM collaboration with crowdsourcing mechanisms and suggest the opportunities for multi-domain RAG knowledge enhancement and vertical domain LLM training with Federated Learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "QualBench," a new benchmark for evaluating Chinese Large Language Models (LLMs) based on localized professional qualification exams. This benchmark aims to address the limitations of existing benchmarks that often lack coverage in vertical domains specific to the Chinese working context. The QualBench dataset includes over 17,000 questions across six vertical domains, grounded in 24 Chinese qualifications.  The paper details the data construction process, presents evaluation results of several LLMs (including both Chinese and non-Chinese models), and discusses challenges and potential future research directions such as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) and Federated Learning for vertical domain LLM training. The results highlight the importance of localized domain knowledge, as Chinese LLMs generally outperformed non-Chinese LLMs on this benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *specific focus* on localized, domain-specific professional qualifications within the Chinese context.  While the general idea of using exam data to evaluate LLMs is not entirely new (several existing benchmarks listed in Table 2 do this), QualBench distinguishes itself by focusing on a specific, under-represented niche (Chinese qualifications across multiple vertical domains) and highlighting the limitations of non-localized knowledge. The introduction of a multi-domain benchmark, rather than a benchmark focusing on a single domain, also adds a layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing importance of LLMs in real-world applications, particularly in regions with unique regulatory landscapes and professional standards, such as China. By providing a benchmark aligned with these localized requirements, QualBench contributes to a more realistic assessment of LLM capabilities for deployment in practical scenarios.  It also provides valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs in handling domain-specific knowledge within the Chinese context. The exploration of challenges in multi-domain RAG and the suggestion of Federated Learning for domain-specific training are potentially significant contributions. However, the impact of these subroutines remains theoretical as the paper only explores the challenges.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the need for localized, domain-specific evaluation of LLMs within the Chinese context.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** The dataset is of reasonable size and covers diverse vertical domains. The data construction process and the inclusion of domain expert verification enhance the dataset's reliability.
    *   **Comparative analysis:** The evaluation of both Chinese and non-Chinese LLMs provides a useful comparison and underscores the importance of localized knowledge.
    *   **Discussion of future directions:** The paper explores and analyzes potential directions for knowledge enhancement, even if the actual experiments remain limited.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Marginal performance:** The best performance achieved (75.26%) indicates significant room for improvement, and the paper primarily reveals limitations of the evaluated models rather than a solution.
    *   **LLM Crowdsourcing Failure:** The failure of LLM crowdsourcing to outperform a single best model is concerning. This point is highlighted, but the paper does not thoroughly investigate why these LLMs can not be effectively aggregated.
    *   **Limited experiments on proposed subroutines:** RAG and Federated Learning are discussed as potential future directions, but they are not experimentally evaluated in the paper. This limits the impact of these suggestions. The paper describes the problem and analyzes the challenges of knowledge enhancement but falls short of empirically demonstrating concrete solutions.
    *   **Potential Data Contamination:** The paper identifies the risk of data contamination in training sets of Chinese LLMs, highlighting the lack of control of data. The performance may be inflated due to existing training data containing the test data.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing a new, localized benchmark for Chinese LLMs, its novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking. The key contribution lies in the dataset itself and the analysis of existing models, not in the development of novel evaluation methodologies or substantial improvements in LLM performance. The limited experimental evaluation of potential solutions weakens the overall impact. The dataset may also be contaminated with existing training data.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ChemRxivQuest: A Curated Chemistry Question-Answer Database Extracted from ChemRxiv Preprints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05232v1)
- **Authors**: Mahmoud Amiri, Thomas Bocklitz
- **Abstract**: The rapid expansion of chemistry literature poses significant challenges for researchers seeking to efficiently access domain-specific knowledge. To support advancements in chemistry-focused natural language processing (NLP), we present ChemRxivQuest, a curated dataset of 970 high-quality question-answer (QA) pairs derived from 155 ChemRxiv preprints across 17 subfields of chemistry. Each QA pair is explicitly linked to its source text segment to ensure traceability and contextual accuracy. ChemRxivQuest was constructed using an automated pipeline that combines optical character recognition (OCR), GPT-4o-based QA generation, and a fuzzy matching technique for answer verification. The dataset emphasizes conceptual, mechanistic, applied, and experimental questions, enabling applications in retrieval-based QA systems, search engine development, and fine-tuning of domain-adapted large language models. We analyze the dataset's structure, coverage, and limitations, and outline future directions for expansion and expert validation. ChemRxivQuest provides a foundational resource for chemistry NLP research, education, and tool development.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper "ChemRxivQuest: A Curated Chemistry Question-Answer Database Extracted from ChemRxiv Preprints".

**Summary**

The paper introduces ChemRxivQuest, a new dataset comprising 970 validated question-answer (QA) pairs extracted from 155 ChemRxiv preprints spanning 17 chemistry subfields. The dataset aims to address the lack of high-quality, structured resources for training and evaluating chemistry-specific natural language processing (NLP) models. The construction pipeline involves optical character recognition (OCR) of the preprints, automated QA pair generation using OpenAI's GPT-4o model, and a fuzzy matching technique for answer verification. The authors analyze the dataset's structure, coverage, and limitations, and discuss its potential applications in areas such as retrieval-based chemistry search engines, fine-tuning large language models, and benchmarking NLP models for scientific question answering. The paper concludes by outlining future work to improve the dataset's quality and utility, including incorporating human expert validation and expanding the data sources.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Clear Gap:** The paper directly tackles a recognized need for high-quality, chemistry-specific datasets for NLP tasks. The lack of such resources has been a bottleneck in advancing chemistry-focused AI, so this contribution is timely and relevant.
    *   **Comprehensive Pipeline:** The dataset construction pipeline is well-defined and reproducible. The combination of OCR, automated QA generation, and fuzzy matching answer verification seems reasonable, offering a balance between automation and quality control. The detailed explanation of each step enhances transparency.
    *   **Dataset Diversity:** The dataset attempts to cover a broad range of chemistry subfields and question types (conceptual, mechanistic, applied, and synthetic/experimental). This diversity is a significant advantage, enabling a wider range of applications.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The paper clearly articulates several practical applications for the dataset, including building better chemistry search engines, fine-tuning domain-specific language models, and benchmarking scientific QA systems. These potential uses increase the dataset's impact.
    *   **Reproducible Methodology:** The paper emphasises a rigorous methodology, which increases the likelihood that other researchers can build upon this dataset.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Preprints:** The sole use of ChemRxiv preprints as a data source is a significant concern. Preprints have not undergone peer review, which raises questions about the accuracy and reliability of the information. Claims and mechanistic insights presented in these preprints may be revised, corrected, or even retracted upon peer review. Although the paper acknowledges this limitation, the impact on dataset quality should not be underestimated.
    *   **Automated QA Generation and Hallucinations:** While the use of GPT-4o for QA generation is understandable, it introduces the risk of model hallucinations and factual inaccuracies. The fuzzy matching verification method, while helpful, may not catch all instances of incorrect or misleading answers.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of QA Accuracy:** While the paper mentions manual review of a small subset of the QA pairs, it doesn't provide detailed information on the error rate or the types of errors found. A more thorough evaluation of the QA accuracy would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Semantic verification method:** The choice of fuzzy string matching prioritizes textual similarity over semantic correctness. As a result, syntactically accurate but contextually invalid answers may inadvertently be accepted into the final dataset.
    *   **Bias in Subfield Distribution:** The uneven distribution of QA pairs across chemistry subfields may introduce bias during model training and limit the generalizability of the dataset.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty of the paper lies in the curation and generation of a large, publicly available QA dataset specifically tailored for chemistry NLP. While other scientific QA datasets exist, the focus on chemistry and the use of ChemRxiv preprints make this a unique resource.
    *   The significance of the dataset is its potential to accelerate research in chemistry NLP, particularly in areas such as information retrieval, language model fine-tuning, and scientific question answering. The availability of a high-quality dataset could lower the barrier to entry for researchers and drive innovation in the field.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable resource for the chemistry NLP community. While the reliance on preprints and the potential for model hallucinations are significant limitations, the dataset's size, diversity, and well-defined construction pipeline make it a worthwhile contribution. The described methodology and analyses are reasonably rigorous. Addressing the limitations, particularly by incorporating expert validation and expanding the data sources, would substantially improve the dataset's quality and impact.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

I assigned a score of 6.5 because of the balance between the paper's contributions and limitations. The dataset addresses a recognized need and has the potential to accelerate research in chemistry NLP (worth 2 points). The well-defined construction pipeline and diversity of the dataset are also significant strengths (worth 1 point each). However, the reliance on preprints as a sole data source and the limitations of the automated QA generation and verification process detract from the overall score (minus 1 point each). The semantic matching verification method and the bias in subfield distribution detract somewhat from the overall score (minus 0.5 points each). An 7 would require a higher level of validation of the generated QA-pairs to reduce error rate. Overall, it represents a solid contribution that could be improved with expert verification.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Latte: Transfering LLMs` Latent-level Knowledge for Few-shot Tabular Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05237v1)
- **Authors**: Ruxue Shi, Hengrui Gu, Hangting Ye, Yiwei Dai, Xu Shen, Xin Wang
- **Abstract**: Few-shot tabular learning, in which machine learning models are trained with a limited amount of labeled data, provides a cost-effective approach to addressing real-world challenges. The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has sparked interest in leveraging their pre-trained knowledge for few-shot tabular learning. Despite promising results, existing approaches either rely on test-time knowledge extraction, which introduces undesirable latency, or text-level knowledge, which leads to unreliable feature engineering. To overcome these limitations, we propose Latte, a training-time knowledge extraction framework that transfers the latent prior knowledge within LLMs to optimize a more generalized downstream model. Latte enables general knowledge-guided downstream tabular learning, facilitating the weighted fusion of information across different feature values while reducing the risk of overfitting to limited labeled data. Furthermore, Latte is compatible with existing unsupervised pre-training paradigms and effectively utilizes available unlabeled samples to overcome the performance limitations imposed by an extremely small labeled dataset. Extensive experiments on various few-shot tabular learning benchmarks demonstrate the superior performance of Latte, establishing it as a state-of-the-art approach in this domain
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Latte," a novel framework for few-shot tabular learning that leverages latent-level knowledge from large language models (LLMs).  Unlike existing approaches that rely on test-time knowledge extraction or text-level knowledge, Latte performs training-time knowledge extraction, distilling the latent prior knowledge within LLMs to optimize a downstream tabular learning model.  Latte comprises a semantic-aware tabular encoder (which integrates feature semantics), and a knowledge adapter (which uses latent-level knowledge to weightedly fuse information from feature values). An unsupervised pre-training stage using unlabeled data is also incorporated.  The authors demonstrate Latte's superior performance compared to existing methods across various few-shot tabular learning benchmarks, highlighting its effectiveness and establishing it as a state-of-the-art approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The core idea of using latent-level knowledge from LLMs *during training* to guide a downstream tabular model is a significant step beyond existing methods that either use LLMs at inference time (leading to latency issues) or rely on unreliable text-level knowledge (as in FeatLLM). The design of the knowledge adapter to bridge the semantic gap between LLMs and the tabular encoder is a key novel aspect. The inclusion of a semantic-aware encoder is also notable, as it addresses a known weakness of existing tabular learning methods.  The incorporation of unsupervised pre-training using pseudo-labels generated from clustering is a relatively standard technique, but its integration within the Latte framework enhances its practical utility.

*Significance:* Few-shot tabular learning is a practically important area because of the high cost of data annotation.  LLMs have shown promise but have limitations, which Latte attempts to address. The paper's results demonstrate tangible performance improvements over existing methods. This suggests that the approach has the potential to impact how few-shot tabular learning problems are approached.  The comprehensive ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component. The efficiency gains due to reduced LLM calls are also significant, making the approach more deployable in resource-constrained settings.

*Strengths:*
*   Clear problem formulation and well-defined methodology.
*   Novel use of latent-level knowledge distillation.
*   Semantic-aware encoder specifically designed for tabular data.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple datasets.
*   Detailed ablation studies that validate the design choices.
*   Demonstrated efficiency improvements in terms of LLM calls.

*Weaknesses:*
*   While the use of pseudo-labels is common, the sensitivity to the clustering parameters and the impact of the quality of the pseudo-labels could be investigated further.
*   The paper focuses heavily on performance metrics. A deeper qualitative analysis of the knowledge transferred from the LLM and how it influences the downstream model's decision-making would further strengthen the paper. What specific relationships between features is it picking up?
*   The reliance on metadata (task and feature descriptions) is potentially limiting.  The performance of Latte is dependent on the quality and availability of this metadata. The paper could explore how the system could be made more robust to noisy or incomplete metadata.
*   The performance gains from simply increasing the size of the LLM aren't significant. This suggests there may be a diminishing returns effect. A better understanding of what the LLM contributes beyond a certain size is needed.

*Potential Influence:* Latte has the potential to become a widely adopted approach for few-shot tabular learning. The methodology can be applied to other tasks and domains by choosing the right tabular encoder. The paper might inspire more work in training-time distillation of knowledge from LLMs for structured data, including methods for more robust metadata handling and ways to better extract and represent relevant relationships to downstream models.

Score: 8

*Justification:* Latte presents a significant advance in few-shot tabular learning by strategically leveraging latent-level knowledge from LLMs during training. The novelty lies in the overall architecture and the knowledge adapter design. The comprehensive experimental results, efficiency gains, and ablation studies convincingly demonstrate the framework's effectiveness. The weaknesses primarily concern the reliance on high-quality metadata and the need for a deeper qualitative analysis. However, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses, and it is expected to have a strong influence on future research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### T-T: Table Transformer for Tagging-based Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05271v1)
- **Authors**: Kun Peng, Chaodong Tong, Cong Cao, Hao Peng, Qian Li, Guanlin Wu, Lei Jiang, Yanbing Liu, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: Aspect sentiment triplet extraction (ASTE) aims to extract triplets composed of aspect terms, opinion terms, and sentiment polarities from given sentences. The table tagging method is a popular approach to addressing this task, which encodes a sentence into a 2-dimensional table, allowing for the tagging of relations between any two words. Previous efforts have focused on designing various downstream relation learning modules to better capture interactions between tokens in the table, revealing that a stronger capability to capture relations can lead to greater improvements in the model. Motivated by this, we attempt to directly utilize transformer layers as downstream relation learning modules. Due to the powerful semantic modeling capability of transformers, it is foreseeable that this will lead to excellent improvement. However, owing to the quadratic relation between the length of the table and the length of the input sentence sequence, using transformers directly faces two challenges: overly long table sequences and unfair local attention interaction. To address these challenges, we propose a novel Table-Transformer (T-T) for the tagging-based ASTE method. Specifically, we introduce a stripe attention mechanism with a loop-shift strategy to tackle these challenges. The former modifies the global attention mechanism to only attend to a 2-dimensional local attention window, while the latter facilitates interaction between different attention windows. Extensive and comprehensive experiments demonstrate that the T-T, as a downstream relation learning module, achieves state-of-the-art performance with lower computational costs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel "Table-Transformer" (T-T) architecture designed for aspect sentiment triplet extraction (ASTE) using a table tagging approach. ASTE aims to extract (aspect term, opinion term, sentiment polarity) triplets from sentences. The core problem addressed is the computational inefficiency of applying transformer layers directly to the 2D table structure inherent in the table tagging method. Standard transformer layers have O(n^2) complexity, which explodes to O(n^4) when applied to the n x n table. To combat this, the T-T architecture employs two main strategies:

1.  **Stripe Attention:** A modified attention mechanism that limits attention to a fixed-size "stripe" or window around each token, reducing computational complexity.

2.  **Loop-Shift Strategy:** A cyclical shift operation applied between T-T layers to facilitate interaction between different attention windows, overcoming the limitations of purely local attention.

The paper claims state-of-the-art results on four benchmark ASTE datasets, demonstrating improved performance over previous methods with lower computational costs. Experiments validate the effectiveness of both the stripe attention and loop-shift mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key contributions are the stripe attention and loop-shift mechanisms within the context of table tagging for ASTE. While both sliding window attention and shifting/rolling operations have precedents in other transformer architectures (e.g., Longformer, Swin Transformer), their adaptation and application specifically to the table tagging approach for ASTE appears novel. However, the underlying concepts are not entirely new. The degree of novelty is somewhat incremental, being an adaptation and optimization of existing techniques to a specific problem within ASTE.

*   **Significance:** ASTE is a relevant and active research area in NLP. The problem of computational complexity associated with table tagging is a genuine hurdle. Successfully addressing this with the T-T architecture would be a significant contribution. The experimental results support this claim, showing improvements over existing table tagging methods. However, the paper's significance is partially tempered by the fact that it primarily improves performance *within* the existing paradigm of table tagging. A more groundbreaking contribution might involve questioning or transcending the table tagging paradigm altogether. The computational cost improvement is also important, but needs careful scrutiny, especially when compared against newer methods. It is also possible the advantages are tied to the relatively smaller datasets currently being used, and the benefits would diminish as sequence length grows.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-described architecture and strategies.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on standard datasets.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the impact of the proposed mechanisms.
    *   Addresses a practical challenge (computational cost) in a relevant NLP task.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty of the core techniques (stripe attention, loop-shift) is somewhat incremental, building upon existing ideas.
    *   The evaluation focuses mainly on established benchmarks. It is unclear how these improvements will translate to significantly more complex tasks.
    *   The computational cost analysis could be more rigorous, comparing against a wider range of recent efficient transformer methods. Including parameter count and FLOPS would have strengthened this aspect of the evaluation.
    *   The gains are compared mostly within the table-tagging paradigm, not challenging this approach in its totality.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on table tagging for ASTE by offering a more efficient and effective way to model relationships within the table structure. The stripe attention and loop-shift strategies could be adopted or adapted in other table-based NLP tasks as well. However, its influence is limited by the fact that it optimizes an existing approach rather than revolutionizing it.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a competent and well-executed solution to a relevant problem in ASTE. The T-T architecture, with its stripe attention and loop-shift strategies, provides a demonstrable improvement in performance and efficiency over existing table tagging methods. However, the novelty of the core techniques is somewhat incremental, adapting existing concepts rather than introducing entirely new ones. Also, the significance of the improvement, while clear within the specific context of table tagging, does not fundamentally alter the trajectory of the ASTE field. Therefore, it warrants a score in the mid-range, reflecting a solid but not transformative contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Software Development Life Cycle Perspective: A Survey of Benchmarks for CodeLLMs and Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05283v1)
- **Authors**: Kaixin Wang, Tianlin Li, Xiaoyu Zhang, Chong Wang, Weisong Sun, Yang Liu, Bin Shi
- **Abstract**: Code large language models (CodeLLMs) and agents have shown great promise in tackling complex software engineering tasks.Compared to traditional software engineering methods, CodeLLMs and agents offer stronger abilities, and can flexibly process inputs and outputs in both natural and code. Benchmarking plays a crucial role in evaluating the capabilities of CodeLLMs and agents, guiding their development and deployment. However, despite their growing significance, there remains a lack of comprehensive reviews of benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents. To bridge this gap, this paper provides a comprehensive review of existing benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents, studying and analyzing 181 benchmarks from 461 relevant papers, covering the different phases of the software development life cycle (SDLC). Our findings reveal a notable imbalance in the coverage of current benchmarks, with approximately 60% focused on the software development phase in SDLC, while requirements engineering and software design phases receive minimal attention at only 5% and 3%, respectively. Additionally, Python emerges as the dominant programming language across the reviewed benchmarks. Finally, this paper highlights the challenges of current research and proposes future directions, aiming to narrow the gap between the theoretical capabilities of CodeLLMs and agents and their application in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of benchmarks for Code Large Language Models (CodeLLMs) and agents from a Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC) perspective. It analyzes 181 benchmarks from 461 relevant papers, categorizing them into phases of the SDLC (requirements engineering, design, development, testing, and maintenance). The survey reveals an imbalance in benchmark coverage, with a strong emphasis on the software development phase and limited attention to requirements engineering and software design. Python is identified as the dominant programming language across the reviewed benchmarks. The paper discusses the challenges of current research and proposes future directions to bridge the gap between theoretical capabilities of CodeLLMs/agents and their real-world application.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in providing a comprehensive review of CodeLLM and agent benchmarks through the lens of the SDLC. While there are existing surveys, this work's SDLC-centric approach, focus on CodeLLM agents, and broad coverage of benchmarks represent a significant contribution. It synthesizes a large body of work and offers a structured view of the evaluation landscape.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it highlights critical gaps in the current benchmarking ecosystem. The imbalance in SDLC coverage raises concerns about the comprehensive evaluation of CodeLLMs and agents, particularly their ability to handle complex software engineering tasks that span multiple phases. The identification of Python's dominance and the lack of benchmarks for other languages also points to a need for greater diversity. By identifying these gaps and proposing future research directions, the paper provides valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a substantial number of benchmarks and papers, providing a broad overview of the field.
    *   **Structured Approach:** The SDLC-based categorization offers a clear and organized framework for analysis.
    *   **Gap Identification:** The paper effectively identifies imbalances and limitations in current benchmark coverage.
    *   **Future Directions:** The proposed future research directions are insightful and address key challenges in the evaluation of CodeLLMs and agents.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the survey covers a large number of benchmarks, the depth of analysis for each individual benchmark could be improved.
    *   **Subjectivity in Categorization:** Categorizing benchmarks into specific SDLC phases may involve some degree of subjectivity, and alternative interpretations are possible.
    *   **Practical Guidance:** While the paper provides future directions, more concrete recommendations for benchmark design and construction could be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   Guiding researchers towards under-explored areas of CodeLLM/agent evaluation.
    *   Encouraging the development of more comprehensive and realistic benchmarks.
    *   Promoting greater diversity in benchmark design, including support for different programming languages and SDLC phases.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a valuable overview and critical assessment of existing benchmarks for CodeLLMs and agents. The SDLC perspective is insightful, and the identification of gaps and future directions contributes to the advancement of the field. However, the analysis could be deepened.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL: Optimizing LLM Inference Request Scheduling for Agentic Text-to-SQL Workflow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05286v1)
- **Authors**: You Peng, Youhe Jiang, Chen Wang, Binhang Yuan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in leveraging the agentic paradigm of large language models (LLMs) utilization have significantly enhanced Text-to-SQL capabilities, enabling users without specialized database expertise to query data intuitively. However, deploying these agentic LLM-based Text-to-SQL systems in production poses substantial challenges due to their inherently multi-stage workflows, stringent latency constraints, and potentially heterogeneous GPU infrastructure in enterprise environments. Current LLM serving frameworks lack effective mechanisms for handling interdependent inference tasks, dynamic latency variability, and resource heterogeneity, leading to suboptimal performance and frequent service-level objective (SLO) violations. In this paper, we introduce HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL, a novel framework designed explicitly to schedule and execute agentic multi-stage LLM-based Text-to-SQL workflows on heterogeneous GPU clusters that handle multi-tenant end-to-end queries. HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL introduce a hierarchical scheduling approach combining global workload-balanced task dispatching and local adaptive urgency-guided prioritization, guided by a systematic analysis of agentic Text-to-SQL workflows. Additionally, we propose a lightweight simulation-based method for tuning critical scheduling hyperparameters, further enhancing robustness and adaptability. Our extensive evaluation on realistic Text-to-SQL benchmarks demonstrates that HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art LLM serving frameworks. Specifically, HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL reduces latency deadlines by up to 1.67$\times$ (average: 1.41$\times$) and improves system throughput by up to 1.75$\times$ (average: 1.65$\times$) compared to vLLM under diverse, realistic workload conditions. Our code is available at https://github.com/Relaxed-System-Lab/Hexgen-Flow.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL: Optimizing LLM Inference Request Scheduling for Agentic Text-to-SQL Workflows":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL, a novel framework designed to optimize the scheduling and execution of agentic, multi-stage LLM-based Text-to-SQL workflows in heterogeneous GPU clusters. It addresses the challenges arising from the inherent complexity of these workflows, stringent latency constraints, and resource heterogeneity. HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL employs a hierarchical scheduling approach, combining global workload-balanced task dispatching with local adaptive urgency-guided prioritization. The framework also includes a lightweight simulation-based method for tuning scheduling hyperparameters. Experimental results on realistic Text-to-SQL benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements in latency and throughput compared to state-of-the-art LLM serving frameworks like vLLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a relevant and increasingly important problem: optimizing the serving infrastructure for agentic LLM-based applications, specifically focusing on Text-to-SQL. The challenges highlighted (dependency management, heterogeneity of requests and resources, and SLO constraints) are well-articulated and reflect real-world deployment concerns.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem and its challenges, emphasizing the limitations of existing LLM serving frameworks in handling complex, multi-stage workflows.
*   **Design Principles:** The proposed design principles (explicit multi-stage dependency management, heterogeneity-aware request allocation, and SLO guarantees) are sound and directly address the identified challenges.
*   **Hierarchical Scheduling:** The hierarchical scheduling approach is a key strength. Combining global workload balancing with local priority queuing allows for both efficient resource utilization and effective handling of SLOs. The adaptive urgency-guided prioritization seems particularly well-suited to managing dependencies and deadlines in multi-stage workflows.
*   **Simulation-Based Tuning:** The inclusion of a simulation-based hyperparameter tuning method is a valuable addition. It enhances the robustness and adaptability of the framework across diverse workload patterns.
*   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experiments are comprehensive and use realistic Text-to-SQL benchmarks. The comparison against vLLM and ablation studies provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL. The reported improvements in latency and throughput are significant.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow. The design choices are clearly explained and justified.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:**  While the combination of techniques is well-executed, the individual components (workload balancing, priority queuing) are not entirely novel in the broader scheduling literature. The novelty primarily lies in their application and integration within the specific context of agentic Text-to-SQL workflows and their tuning for LLM inference.
*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the Text-to-SQL application is relevant, the evaluation could benefit from including other agentic LLM-based application, to generalize the efficacy of the proposed method.
*   **Dependency on Existing Allocation System:** The paper mentions they assume that the allocation of multiple model instances can be effectively determined by existing systems, without providing an explicit implementation of allocation. This assumption could introduce performance bottlenecks.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of work on optimizing LLM serving infrastructure. By focusing on agentic workflows and addressing the specific challenges of Text-to-SQL, HEXGEN-TEXT2SQL offers a practical and effective solution for improving the performance and reliability of these systems in real-world deployments. The framework's ability to handle heterogeneous resources and meet stringent SLOs makes it particularly valuable for enterprise environments.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-designed and evaluated framework that addresses a relevant problem in the field of LLM serving. The hierarchical scheduling approach and simulation-based tuning are valuable contributions. However, the incremental nature of some of the individual components and the reliance on an existing allocation system prevent it from achieving a higher score. Although I consider the topic important, the incremental novelty of the solution leads to a slightly lower assessment. The detailed evaluation and practical applicability of the framework justify a score of 7.5, indicating a solid and worthwhile contribution to the field but with room for further innovation.

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking Ophthalmology Foundation Models for Clinically Significant Age Macular Degeneration Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05291v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin A. Cohen, Jonathan Fhima, Meishar Meisel, Baskin Meital, Luis Filipe Nakayama, Eran Berkowitz, Joachim A. Behar
- **Abstract**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) has enabled Vision Transformers (ViTs) to learn robust representations from large-scale natural image datasets, enhancing their generalization across domains. In retinal imaging, foundation models pretrained on either natural or ophthalmic data have shown promise, but the benefits of in-domain pretraining remain uncertain. To investigate this, we benchmark six SSL-pretrained ViTs on seven digital fundus image (DFI) datasets totaling 70,000 expert-annotated images for the task of moderate-to-late age-related macular degeneration (AMD) identification. Our results show that iBOT pretrained on natural images achieves the highest out-of-distribution generalization, with AUROCs of 0.80-0.97, outperforming domain-specific models, which achieved AUROCs of 0.78-0.96 and a baseline ViT-L with no pretraining, which achieved AUROCs of 0.68-0.91. These findings highlight the value of foundation models in improving AMD identification and challenge the assumption that in-domain pretraining is necessary. Furthermore, we release BRAMD, an open-access dataset (n=587) of DFIs with AMD labels from Brazil.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper benchmarks several state-of-the-art Vision Transformer (ViT) foundation models, pre-trained on both natural and ophthalmic images, for the task of identifying intermediate-to-late Age-related Macular Degeneration (AMD) in digital fundus images (DFIs).  The authors evaluated six pre-trained ViTs on seven DFI datasets (including a new, publicly released dataset called BRAMD) totaling around 70,000 images.  Their results suggest that a ViT pre-trained on natural images (iBOT) achieves the highest out-of-distribution generalization performance.  They then fine-tune the iBOT model using a multi-source domain training approach, resulting in a new model called AMDNet. AMDNet is then compared to a state-of-the-art AMD detection model called DeepSeeNet. The paper concludes that general foundation models outperform domain-specific ones and that AMDNet demonstrates robust and high OOD generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While the application of existing foundation models to AMD detection isn't entirely new, the thoroughness of the benchmark is a strong point. Comparing both general and domain-specific pre-trained models on a large, diverse set of datasets is valuable. The finding that general foundation models outperform in-domain pre-trained models *challenges the prevailing wisdom* in the field. The release of the BRAMD dataset is a useful contribution to the community. The use of multi-source domain training to improve OOD generalization is also valuable. However, the methodology itself (fine-tuning pre-trained models and using multi-source domain adaptation) is not radically novel.

* **Significance:** The paper has several significant implications:
    *   **Challenges the dominance of in-domain pretraining:**  The finding that natural image pre-training is superior to retinal image pre-training for generalization is a major finding. This could shift the focus of future research away from creating specialized ophthalmic foundation models and towards more effective fine-tuning and adaptation strategies for general models.
    *   **Provides a valuable benchmark:**  The extensive benchmarking across multiple datasets and models provides a clear comparison point for future research in this area.
    *   **Releases a new dataset:**  The BRAMD dataset helps alleviate the scarcity of labeled retinal datasets, furthering research in this area.
    *   **Offers a new state-of-the-art model:** The model developed, AMDNet, outperforms the baselines provided.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is very thorough, covering a wide range of models and datasets. The OOD evaluation is particularly strong.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its findings.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Releasing the dataset and model details (AMDNet) increases reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited novelty of methods:** The techniques used for fine-tuning and domain adaptation are relatively standard. The major novelty lies in the findings and implications, not the methods themselves.
    *   **Limited analysis of failure modes:** While the error analysis includes demographic biases and comorbidity effects, a deeper dive into specific image characteristics that cause misclassifications would be beneficial. Examples showing difficult cases where AMDNet fails would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **No comparison against other potential SOTA models beyond DeepSeeNet:** While DeepSeeNet is useful, comparing against other modern architectures would allow a more holistic view of how the model compares to the rest of the field.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could significantly influence the direction of research in ophthalmic AI. The emphasis on general foundation models and OOD generalization is likely to become more prominent. The BRAMD dataset will also be a valuable resource for researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   It is significantly innovative, however it doesn't radically create a new method.
*   It does fill a gap in understanding OOD generalization in AMD detection.
*   The contribution of the BRAMD dataset is valuable to the community.
*   The findings are clearly presented, and the benchmark is thorough.

However, the methods used are not especially novel.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward Reasonable Parrots: Why Large Language Models Should Argue with Us by Design
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05298v1)
- **Authors**: Elena Musi, Nadin Kokciyan, Khalid Al-Khatib, Davide Ceolin, Emmanuelle Dietz, Klara Gutekunst, Annette Hautli-Janisz, Cristian Manuel Santibañez Yañez, Jodi Schneider, Jonas Scholz, Cor Steging, Jacky Visser, Henning Wachsmuth
- **Abstract**: In this position paper, we advocate for the development of conversational technology that is inherently designed to support and facilitate argumentative processes. We argue that, at present, large language models (LLMs) are inadequate for this purpose, and we propose an ideal technology design aimed at enhancing argumentative skills. This involves re-framing LLMs as tools to exercise our critical thinking rather than replacing them. We introduce the concept of 'reasonable parrots' that embody the fundamental principles of relevance, responsibility, and freedom, and that interact through argumentative dialogical moves. These principles and moves arise out of millennia of work in argumentation theory and should serve as the starting point for LLM-based technology that incorporates basic principles of argumentation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Large Language Models (LLMs) should be designed to actively engage in argumentative processes with users, rather than simply providing answers or mimicking human-like responses. The authors contend that current LLMs, which they characterize as "stochastic parrots," are inadequate for fostering critical thinking and deliberation. They propose a new design paradigm centered around "reasonable parrots," LLMs that embody principles of relevance, responsibility, and freedom, and interact through argumentative dialogical moves.  The paper draws on argumentation theory to suggest a multi-parrot approach, with each "parrot" persona embodying a distinct argumentative role (Socratic, Cynical, Eclectic, Aristotelian) to challenge the user's views and encourage deeper reflection.  The paper includes a prompt-based exploration of how this multi-parrot approach might work in practice.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core idea, that LLMs should be designed to *argue* with users to promote critical thinking, is relatively novel. While previous work has explored using LLMs for debate or generating arguments, the explicit focus on designing systems to *facilitate the argumentative process itself* as a means of improving user's thinking is a distinct contribution. The framing of LLMs as "reasonable parrots," designed according to argumentation principles, also offers a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The topic is highly relevant given the increasing reliance on LLMs for information access and decision-making. The potential for LLMs to reinforce biases or propagate misinformation is a significant concern. The paper directly addresses this by advocating for a design that actively challenges users' assumptions and encourages more thorough consideration of different viewpoints. This has potential implications for improving the quality of human reasoning in the age of AI.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a significant problem: the potential for LLMs to inhibit rather than enhance critical thinking.
    *   **Well-Grounded in Theory:** The paper draws effectively on argumentation theory to provide a solid theoretical foundation for its proposed design.
    *   **Concrete Examples:** The multi-parrot example, while preliminary, provides a tangible illustration of the paper's core concept.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Implementation Details:** The paper remains at a relatively high level of abstraction. It lacks concrete details on how the "reasonable parrot" design would be technically implemented, particularly in terms of ensuring adherence to the principles of relevance, responsibility, and freedom.
    *   **Limited Empirical Evaluation:** The multi-parrot example is based on prompt engineering of current LLMs and it falls short of being a controlled user study or a robust evaluation of the approach's effectiveness. The anecdotes provide insights into some of the capabilities and challenges in instantiating dialogue.
    *   **Oversimplification of Argumentation:** While the paper draws on argumentation theory, the proposed personas and dialogical moves are somewhat simplified. Real-world argumentation is far more complex, involving nuanced interactions, emotional factors, and rhetorical strategies beyond the scope of the proposed framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future LLM-based conversational systems. It could stimulate research on incorporating argumentation principles into AI design and on evaluating the impact of AI systems on human reasoning skills. The concept of "reasonable parrots" could become a useful framework for thinking about AI ethics and responsible innovation. The initial prompt examples have a lot of the issues that one might expect and the multi-parrot persona may have utility in training users to identify fallacies, but the authors will need to demonstrate its utility with human participants.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a compelling argument and a novel design concept, its lack of implementation detail and empirical validation limits its overall impact. The multi-parrot prompt examples are promising, but they require more rigorous evaluation and refinement. The oversimplification of complex dynamics and argument structure also affects the credibility of the proposal. Given these factors, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field, but with significant room for improvement.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICon: In-Context Contribution for Automatic Data Selection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05327v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Yang, Qingxiu Dong, Linli Yao, Fangwei Zhu, Zhifang Sui
- **Abstract**: Data selection for instruction tuning is essential for improving the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) and reducing training cost. However, existing automated selection methods either depend on computationally expensive gradient-based measures or manually designed heuristics, which may fail to fully exploit the intrinsic attributes of data. In this paper, we propose In-context Learning for Contribution Measurement (ICon), a novel gradient-free method that takes advantage of the implicit fine-tuning nature of in-context learning (ICL) to measure sample contribution without gradient computation or manual indicators engineering. ICon offers a computationally efficient alternative to gradient-based methods and reduces human inductive bias inherent in heuristic-based approaches. ICon comprises three components and identifies high-contribution data by assessing performance shifts under implicit learning through ICL. Extensive experiments on three LLMs across 12 benchmarks and 5 pairwise evaluation sets demonstrate the effectiveness of ICon. Remarkably, on LLaMA3.1-8B, models trained on 15% of ICon-selected data outperform full datasets by 5.42% points and exceed the best performance of widely used selection methods by 2.06% points. We further analyze high-contribution samples selected by ICon, which show both diverse tasks and appropriate difficulty levels, rather than just the hardest ones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ICON (In-Context Contribution for Measurement), a novel data selection method for instruction tuning of large language models (LLMs). ICON leverages the implicit fine-tuning nature of in-context learning (ICL) to measure the contribution of individual data samples without relying on computationally expensive gradient-based methods or manually designed heuristics.  ICON comprises three components: an assessment set, a contribution score based on perplexity changes under ICL, and a selection paradigm trained with LoRA.  Experiments on LLaMA3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-3B, and LLaMA2-7B across various benchmarks demonstrate that models trained on ICON-selected subsets outperform those trained on full datasets, reducing training costs and even surpassing widely used data selection techniques. The paper also analyzes the characteristics of the selected high-contribution samples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of using ICL as a proxy for measuring the contribution of training samples is relatively novel in the context of automated data selection for instruction tuning. Existing methods primarily rely on gradients or manually engineered features. Bypassing the need for explicit gradient computation through ICL, and training a separate selection classifier, does introduce a computationally efficient alternative.

*   **Significance:** The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of ICON. Consistently outperforming models trained on the full dataset and several existing data selection methods suggests a significant improvement in training efficiency and final model performance. The ability to achieve superior results with significantly reduced computational resources (FLOPs) makes ICON a valuable tool. The analysis of high-contribution samples providing insights into the properties of effective training data further enhances the paper's impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper presents extensive experiments on multiple models and datasets, providing solid evidence for ICON's effectiveness.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:**  Avoiding gradient computation and leveraging LoRA for selection training makes ICON significantly more efficient than gradient-based approaches.
    *   **Addresses Limitations of Existing Methods:**  ICON directly measures sample contribution, mitigating the human inductive bias inherent in heuristic-based methods.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply presenting results and provides valuable insights into the characteristics of high-contribution samples and the optimal data scale for instruction tuning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on a Good Assessment Set:** The performance of ICON hinges on the quality and representativeness of the assessment set. Constructing this set could be challenging in practice and may introduce a degree of human bias, although the paper addresses this by sampling from multiple diverse sources.
    *   **Limited Generalization Analysis of the Selection classifier:** While the paper shows a robustness test transferring the selection paradigm to different datasets, it would be helpful to know what datasets are required to initialize it, or perform zero-shot transfer.
    *   **The LoRA selection paradigm performance:** While LoRA greatly increases the inference performance for finding the ICON samples, how does this performance impact the overall selection performance when a different base model and a different dataset is introduced?

*   **Potential Influence:** ICON has the potential to significantly influence the field of instruction tuning and LLM training by:

    *   Enabling more efficient use of training data.
    *   Facilitating the training of high-performance models with limited computational resources.
    *   Providing a framework for better understanding the properties of effective training data.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

ICON is a strong paper that introduces a novel and effective data selection method for instruction tuning. The use of ICL as a proxy for measuring sample contribution is a creative idea, and the empirical results convincingly demonstrate the benefits of ICON. While there are some limitations related to the dependency on the assessment set and potential for overreliance on a select range, its strengths in performance and computational efficiency make it a notable contribution that addresses significant challenges in the field and unlocks further avenues for improving dataset selection. Overall, I believe ICON is likely to have a substantial impact on the future of instruction tuning.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models for Coastal Inundation Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05381v1)
- **Authors**: Kazi Ashik Islam, Zakaria Mehrab, Mahantesh Halappanavar, Henning Mortveit, Sridhar Katragadda, Jon Derek Loftis, Madhav Marathe
- **Abstract**: Coastal flooding poses significant risks to communities, necessitating fast and accurate forecasting methods to mitigate potential damage. To approach this problem, we present DIFF-FLOOD, a probabilistic spatiotemporal forecasting method designed based on denoising diffusion models. DIFF-FLOOD predicts inundation level at a location by taking both spatial and temporal context into account. It utilizes inundation levels at neighboring locations and digital elevation data as spatial context. Inundation history from a context time window, together with additional co-variates are used as temporal context. Convolutional neural networks and cross-attention mechanism are then employed to capture the spatiotemporal dynamics in the data. We trained and tested DIFF-FLOOD on coastal inundation data from the Eastern Shore of Virginia, a region highly impacted by coastal flooding. Our results show that, DIFF-FLOOD outperforms existing forecasting methods in terms of prediction performance (6% to 64% improvement in terms of two performance metrics) and scalability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DIFF-FLOOD, a novel spatiotemporal forecasting method for coastal inundation based on denoising diffusion models. It aims to address the limitations of traditional physics-based models (PBMs), which are computationally expensive, and existing deep learning methods that struggle to incorporate spatial contexts effectively or scale to high-resolution tasks. DIFF-FLOOD predicts inundation levels by considering both spatial (neighboring inundation levels, digital elevation data) and temporal contexts (inundation history, covariates like tide cycles).  The model employs convolutional neural networks and cross-attention mechanisms to capture the complex spatiotemporal dynamics. Experiments on coastal inundation data from the Eastern Shore of Virginia demonstrate that DIFF-FLOOD outperforms existing methods in terms of prediction performance (Normalized Average Continuous Ranked Probability Score, Normalized Root Mean Square Error) and scalability. The paper also shows how the model can be used to answer scenario-based queries relevant to policymakers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying diffusion models to coastal inundation forecasting with an emphasis on both spatial and temporal contexts is a significant strength.  While diffusion models have been used for time-series forecasting, the specific adaptation to this spatiotemporal prediction problem, combined with the architecture choices to handle the data, represents a novel contribution. Furthermore, the emphasis on scalability is vital, and the results presented suggest progress in this area. The cross-attention mechanism specifically designed for combining temporal and spatial information within a UNet architecture also introduces a new and valuable architectural design.

*   **Significance:** Coastal inundation is a pressing problem with significant societal and economic impacts, making the problem itself highly relevant. The development of a fast, accurate, and scalable forecasting tool has direct practical value. The ability to address scenario-based queries further enhances the significance, potentially aiding policy decisions. The experimental results are convincing, demonstrating improvements over established baselines.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of coastal inundation forecasting in a spatiotemporal context.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of denoising diffusion models combined with convolutional networks and cross-attention for this particular task is innovative.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments convincingly demonstrate the superiority of DIFF-FLOOD over existing methods in terms of prediction accuracy and scalability. The ablation study provides insights into the importance of different components of the model.
    *   **Policy Relevance:** The capability to answer scenario-based queries is highly valuable for policymakers and stakeholders.
    *   **Scalability Focus:** Addressing the scalability issue of other diffusion models for this task.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** While the baselines included are relevant, it could benefit from comparisons to more diverse types of spatial models (e.g., those from the geostatistics literature, or specialized land-surface models that do use machine learning).
    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The model is trained and tested on a single dataset (Eastern Shore of Virginia). Generalizability to other coastal regions with different geographical characteristics and data availability needs to be addressed.
    *   **Computational Cost (Diffusion Models):** While the paper emphasizes scalability compared to *other* diffusion models, denoising diffusion models are inherently computationally intensive during sampling/inference. The runtime performance needs to be reported more explicitly and compared with computationally lighter, though perhaps less accurate, methods.
    *   **Covariate Selection:**  The choice of temporal covariates (hour of day, day of month) is reasonable, but a deeper discussion of why these specific covariates were chosen and how they impact performance would strengthen the paper.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more accurate and scalable coastal inundation forecasting tools. The architecture and the emphasis on scalability have the potential to be adapted in other spatial temporal forecasting scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7 to this paper. While the paper has some weaknesses, it offers a novel and effective approach to a significant problem. The strengths, including the strong experimental results, the focus on scalability, and the potential for policy relevance, outweigh the weaknesses. The paper convincingly demonstrates the superiority of DIFF-FLOOD over existing methods and contributes meaningfully to the field of spatiotemporal forecasting. The potential impact is considerable. It does not reach higher scores due to the limited diversity in baselines, some dataset dependency, and some open issues about computational complexity of running the approach at scale.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### PillarMamba: Learning Local-Global Context for Roadside Point Cloud via Hybrid State Space Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05397v1)
- **Authors**: Zhang Zhang, Chao Sun, Chao Yue, Da Wen, Tianze Wang, Jianghao Leng
- **Abstract**: Serving the Intelligent Transport System (ITS) and Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) tasks, roadside perception has received increasing attention in recent years, as it can extend the perception range of connected vehicles and improve traffic safety. However, roadside point cloud oriented 3D object detection has not been effectively explored. To some extent, the key to the performance of a point cloud detector lies in the receptive field of the network and the ability to effectively utilize the scene context. The recent emergence of Mamba, based on State Space Model (SSM), has shaken up the traditional convolution and transformers that have long been the foundational building blocks, due to its efficient global receptive field. In this work, we introduce Mamba to pillar-based roadside point cloud perception and propose a framework based on Cross-stage State-space Group (CSG), called PillarMamba. It enhances the expressiveness of the network and achieves efficient computation through cross-stage feature fusion. However, due to the limitations of scan directions, state space model faces local connection disrupted and historical relationship forgotten. To address this, we propose the Hybrid State-space Block (HSB) to obtain the local-global context of roadside point cloud. Specifically, it enhances neighborhood connections through local convolution and preserves historical memory through residual attention. The proposed method outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on the popular large scale roadside benchmark: DAIR-V2X-I. The code will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PillarMamba: Learning Local-Global Context for Roadside Point Cloud via Hybrid State Space Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PillarMamba, a novel 3D object detection framework designed specifically for roadside point cloud data.  It leverages the State Space Model (SSM) architecture, particularly Mamba, to capture long-range dependencies in the dense roadside point cloud context. The key contributions are: (1) a Cross-stage State-space Group (CSG) to reduce computational burden and enable cross-stage feature fusion, and (2) a Hybrid State-space Block (HSB) which combines local convolution and residual attention to address limitations of SSMs related to local connection disruption and historical memory loss when applied to point cloud data.  The method is evaluated on the DAIR-V2X-I dataset, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a solid effort in adapting the Mamba architecture to a relatively unexplored domain, roadside point cloud perception. The combination of CSG and HSB exhibits novelty in addressing specific limitations imposed by the nature of point cloud data to SSMs (local context loss and historical relationship issues).  While the core Mamba architecture isn't new, the adaptations specifically targeting roadside point cloud data, including the designed structure for a dense feature context, makes this work relatively novel.

*   **Significance:**  Roadside perception is gaining prominence for its role in intelligent transportation systems (ITS) and extending the perception range of connected vehicles. Enhancing 3D object detection in this context is a significant and relevant problem. Improving performance on the DAIR-V2X-I benchmark suggests the framework's potential to impact practical applications. The performance gains over strong pillar-based baselines indicates a valuable advancement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem addressed is timely and relevant.
    *   The proposed CSG and HSB modules address specific challenges of applying SSM to point cloud data in roadside scenarios, highlighting a degree of insight.
    *   The framework achieves performance improvements on a large-scale, real-world benchmark.
    *   The paper offers clear explanations of the methodology and ablation studies that validate the contributions of individual components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper introduces novel adaptations, the core building blocks (SSM, convolutions, attention) are well-established. The incremental value is the specific combination and application to the roadside point cloud domain.
    *   The latency comparison, while showing competitive performance, is not especially impressive. Faster inference times are highly desirable for real-time ITS applications.
    *   The performance gains, while noticeable, are not dramatically higher than competing methods.
    *   The qualitative results provide visual confirmation but could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the specific failure cases the proposed method addresses.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in applying SSMs to different types of sensor data in ITS. The analysis of SSM limitations in point cloud scenarios could encourage further investigation into methods that effectively capture both local and global context.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel adaptation of the Mamba architecture to a timely and significant problem in the field of intelligent transportation systems, i.e., roadside point cloud 3D object detection. It also addresses key challenges and limitations of existing state-space models.

While the performance gains, although present, are not extremely high and the latency could be further improved, the insights and techniques demonstrated by the paper has the potential to influence other researchers in the application of neural network architectures to roadside point cloud data.

Therefore, a score of 7 seems appropriate, accounting for the originality in approach, significance of the problem and experimental results in the chosen benchmark, and in the contribution to advancing a relative nascent research area in intelligent transportations systems, roadside perception.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Frame In, Frame Out: Do LLMs Generate More Biased News Headlines than Humans?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05406v1)
- **Authors**: Valeria Pastorino, Nafise Sadat Moosavi
- **Abstract**: Framing in media critically shapes public perception by selectively emphasizing some details while downplaying others. With the rise of large language models in automated news and content creation, there is growing concern that these systems may introduce or even amplify framing biases compared to human authors. In this paper, we explore how framing manifests in both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned LLM-generated news content. Our analysis reveals that, particularly in politically and socially sensitive contexts, LLMs tend to exhibit more pronounced framing than their human counterparts. In addition, we observe significant variation in framing tendencies across different model architectures, with some models displaying notably higher biases. These findings point to the need for effective post-training mitigation strategies and tighter evaluation frameworks to ensure that automated news content upholds the standards of balanced reporting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) generate news headlines with more framing bias than human authors. The authors compare the framing present in headlines generated by a diverse set of LLMs (both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned) with human-written headlines from the XSum dataset.  The analysis reveals that LLMs tend to exhibit more framing bias, especially in politically and socially sensitive contexts. The study also finds variations in framing tendencies across different model architectures and a correlation between text length and framing presence. The authors argue that current evaluation metrics for LLMs often neglect framing bias, and they advocate for incorporating such metrics to ensure fair and neutral text generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Novel Research Question:**  The paper addresses a timely and relevant question concerning the potential for LLMs to amplify biases in news generation. As LLMs become increasingly integrated into content creation workflows, understanding and mitigating framing bias is critical.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper examines a wide range of LLM architectures, including both out-of-the-box and fine-tuned models.  This broad analysis provides a more robust understanding of framing tendencies across different LLM families. The comparison with human-generated content is essential for establishing a baseline and quantifying the bias introduced by LLMs.
    * **Quantitative Findings:** The study presents quantitative results, including framing rates across different topics and models. The use of statistical tests (t-tests and correlation analysis) strengthens the validity of the findings. The demonstration that the GPT family shows the highest level of bias and that smaller and fine-tuned models show lower levels of bias than other non-fine-tuned bigger models like Claude and Cohere is a really interesting finding.
    * **Focus on Understudied Aspect:** The study correctly points out that framing bias has often been overlooked in standard evaluation benchmarks. Its importance for neutral and fair representation is underscored.
    * **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights the need for mitigation strategies and improved evaluation frameworks to address framing bias in LLM-generated news content.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Framing Detection Methodology:** The study relies on the approach described in Pastorino et al. (2024), using GPT-4.1 nano, GPT-4o, and GPT-3.5-Turbo as jury to detect framing. While justified by previous studies, the use of another LLM for framing detection may introduce its own biases and uncertainties. The paper does not elaborate on the potential limitations of this approach.
    * **XSum Dataset Limitations:** While XSum is a widely used dataset, it primarily focuses on factual news summaries. The results might not generalize to other types of news content, such as opinion pieces or investigative reports, where framing is more prevalent and complex.
    * **Interpretability of Framing:** The paper does not provide in-depth analyses of *how* framing is manifested in LLM-generated text.  Examples of framing techniques (e.g., specific word choices, emphasis on certain aspects) could strengthen the analysis.
    * **Limited Mitigation Strategies:** The paper identifies the problem of framing bias but does not offer concrete solutions or mitigation strategies. While calling for further research in this area is valid, exploring potential approaches (e.g., incorporating de-biasing techniques during fine-tuning) would enhance the paper's impact.
    * **Topic classification**: The process for topic classification seems to be keyword-based, which might have some limitations.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper is relatively novel. It's one of the first studies to systematically investigate and compare framing bias in LLM-generated news content across various model architectures. The emphasis on framing bias as an overlooked aspect of LLM evaluation and the call for improved mitigation strategies contribute meaningfully to the field. While the methodology for framing detection has been established, applying it to a comprehensive set of LLMs in the context of news generation is a valuable contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field due to its novel research question, comprehensive analysis, and practical implications. However, limitations in the framing detection methodology, dataset choice, lack of in-depth analysis of framing techniques, and absence of mitigation strategies detract from its overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Crosslingual Reasoning through Test-Time Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05408v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng-Xin Yong, M. Farid Adilazuarda, Jonibek Mansurov, Ruochen Zhang, Niklas Muennighoff, Carsten Eickhoff, Genta Indra Winata, Julia Kreutzer, Stephen H. Bach, Alham Fikri Aji
- **Abstract**: Reasoning capabilities of large language models are primarily studied for English, even when pretrained models are multilingual. In this work, we investigate to what extent English reasoning finetuning with long chain-of-thoughts (CoTs) can generalize across languages. First, we find that scaling up inference compute for English-centric reasoning language models (RLMs) improves multilingual mathematical reasoning across many languages including low-resource languages, to an extent where they outperform models twice their size. Second, we reveal that while English-centric RLM's CoTs are naturally predominantly English, they consistently follow a quote-and-think pattern to reason about quoted non-English inputs. Third, we discover an effective strategy to control the language of long CoT reasoning, and we observe that models reason better and more efficiently in high-resource languages. Finally, we observe poor out-of-domain reasoning generalization, in particular from STEM to cultural commonsense knowledge, even for English. Overall, we demonstrate the potentials, study the mechanisms and outline the limitations of crosslingual generalization of English reasoning test-time scaling. We conclude that practitioners should let English-centric RLMs reason in high-resource languages, while further work is needed to improve reasoning in low-resource languages and out-of-domain contexts.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can summarize and critically evaluate the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Crosslingual Reasoning through Test-Time Scaling" investigates the effectiveness of applying English-centric reasoning language models (RLMs) to multilingual reasoning tasks, particularly focusing on mathematical reasoning. It examines how increasing inference compute (test-time scaling) impacts the performance of these models across different languages. Key findings include: 1) Scaling up inference compute improves multilingual mathematical reasoning, even outperforming larger models; 2) RLMs exhibit a "quote-and-think" pattern when processing non-English prompts, quoting non-English phrases before reasoning in English; 3) Controlling the reasoning language of RLMs is possible and beneficial, with high-resource languages yielding better performance; and 4) Cross-domain generalization (e.g., from STEM to cultural commonsense) is limited, even for English.
